Show more Apr 20 Claude responded: The Precise Term Delineated assertion types, work blocks, and their distinct functions systematically Delineated assertion types, work blocks, and their distinct functions systematically The Precise Term Every field should hold content of exactly one *assertion type*. This is more precise than "claim" because claim is a subset. An assertion is anything stated as if it were true, intended, required, or decided. The different assertion types answer fundamentally different questions, require different cognitive modes to produce, and feed different downstream work. Claim specifically means a canonical assertion — a statement that something is true within the story's world. But there are assertion types that are not claims, and conflating them produces the data mixing problem you've been diagnosing. The full taxonomy of assertion types present in your accumulated notes: *Canonical claim* — asserts world truth. "The Stagnation began in 930 ALB." "Earth pony magic scales with pride in the crop." "Trimmel was born in 971 as a starving drone." These are the assertions that live in the world mechanics, historical record, and character psychology containers. They can be revised but at any given moment they are the authoritative truth against which design work is calibrated. *Architectural decision* — asserts information structure across the story. "Characters perceive the Stagnation as 1000 years until Chapter 22." "The reader is held in the same ignorance as the characters." This is not a claim about world truth — the world truth is 930 ALB. It is a decision about when and how world truth is withheld and released. It governs many scenes without being a scene design. *Thematic proposition* — asserts what the story argues and what evidence must be deposited for the reader to arrive at that argument independently. "Harmony is not self-sustaining; it requires structural protection from predation." This is not world truth and not information structure. It is an argumentation design. *Design specification* — asserts how a specific scene should produce a specific reader experience. "The reader should perceive AJ's contentment as a hiding place while AJ experiences it as rightness." This is a scene-level decision, not a world truth or a story-level information structure decision. *Obligation or directive* — asserts that work needs to be done. "Research how medieval tally sticks functioned." "Determine the correct order of Astler, Sickleclaw, and Rosewing." These are not assertions about the world or the design. They are flags on unresolved questions. *Generative hypothesis* — a candidate for becoming a canonical claim that has not yet been decided. "Is Mali the embodiment of Aquileian spirit exported to the Tzinacatl?" This is not a claim. It is a question posed to a future decision. *Bibliographic note* — asserts a real-world analogue or inspiration. "Chrysalis's economic scheme parallels the British East India Company and the Opium Wars." This never becomes a canonical claim. It is a frame that informs the design without entering the story's ontology. *Craft observation* — asserts something about how prose should function. "This scene should use deep third with a sensory transition." "The FID here should make the machine gun feel right to AJ." These inform drafting but are not scene-level experience design specifications. Each of these requires a different field because they answer different questions, were produced by different cognitive modes, feed different downstream work, and have different stability properties. A canonical claim is stable until upstream content forces revision. An obligation disappears when discharged. A hypothesis either resolves into a canonical claim or is discarded. A bibliographic note is permanent and unchanging regardless of story revisions. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Work Blocks, Their Functions, and Required Mindsets These are the discrete blocks of work the project requires. They are not strictly sequential — some are ongoing throughout, some can proceed in parallel in different areas of the world, some cannot begin until others reach local stability. They are named by what they produce. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ *Raw Capture* Function: Prevent the loss of ideas, insights, research fragments, design intuitions, and structural hypotheses as they arrive, regardless of their assertion type or canonical status. Mindset: Non-critical and non-committal. The goal is speed and completeness of capture, not quality or organization. Judgment about what the captured content means and where it belongs is explicitly deferred. Skills: The ability to suspend evaluation. The ability to record without editing. Familiarity with frictionless capture paths. Output: Undifferentiated notes tagged only with the subject they appear to relate to, with no assertion type assigned and no canonical status. What this is not: This is not design work, not world-building, not argumentation. It is preservation. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ *Classification* Function: Read accumulated raw capture and determine the assertion type of each piece of content, route it to the correct container, and identify what is a hypothesis versus a claim versus a directive versus a bibliographic note. Mindset: Taxonomic and analytical. The classifier is asking "what kind of thing is this" not "is this correct." The question of correctness belongs to the next block. Skills: Understanding of assertion types with sufficient precision to distinguish them reliably. Ability to read a note and identify which question it is answering. Output: Content routed to typed fields. Obligations flagged as unresolved. Hypotheses marked as undecided. Bibliographic notes separated from canonical claims that reference the same real-world material. What this is not: Classification does not resolve hypotheses or discharge obligations. It identifies them as needing resolution or discharge. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ *World Mechanics Construction* Function: Establish the invariant rules of the physical, biological, magical, and social world that operate independently of any character's knowledge or any story's plot. These are the constraints that the plot cannot violate. Mindset: Systems engineer and physicist. The question being asked is: what is impossible here? What are the invariants? What does one rule imply about adjacent rules? Internal consistency is the primary test. Skills: Systems thinking. Constraint analysis. The ability to follow an implication to its conclusion even when the conclusion is inconvenient for existing design. The willingness to revise when a new area of mechanics makes an existing claim insufficient. Output: Canonical ontological claims. The earth pony magic system. The charitostatic effect. How changeling emotion sense functions. These are stated as facts about the world independent of any character's perception. What this is not: This block does not decide how or when these mechanics are revealed to the reader. That is an architectural decision made in a later block. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ *Causal History Construction* Function: Establish the objective chronological record of events and the causal chains connecting them. This is what actually happened, independent of what any character knows, believes, or perceives about it. Mindset: Historian. The question being asked is: what happened, when, and because of what prior event? Causality is the primary test. An event without a cause is authorial fiat and should be interrogated. Skills: Timeline management. Causality analysis. The ability to distinguish what happened from what characters believe happened. The discipline to document even events that no POV character witnessed and that will never be directly dramatized. Output: Canonical historical claims. The Herzland Republican Revolution of 978. The specific sequence of Chrysalis's economic consolidation. The 930 ALB trigger for Celestia's revised policy. These are stated as objective facts regardless of character knowledge. What this is not: This block does not decide narrative sequence — the order in which events are revealed to the reader. That is an architectural decision. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ *Character Psychology Construction* Function: Establish who each character is and why, independent of the story events. This means documenting their psychological architecture: the wound, the belief system built around the wound, the epistemic limits that follow from that belief system, and the specific things they cannot perceive because of it. Mindset: Psychologist and biographer. The question being asked is: given this character's formation — their biology, history, and environment — what is the necessary structure of their inner life? This is not a decision about what would be useful for the plot. It is a derivation from the character's established history. Skills: Psychological modeling. The ability to follow a character's wound to its behavioral and perceptual consequences. Understanding of how epistemic limits function — what a character cannot see because of what they believe. Output: Canonical psychological claims. Applejack's core wound is suppressed legitimate ambition. Twilight's core wound is looking to an external authority for conscience. Luna's passivity is self-knowledge rather than guilt. These are stated as character truths independent of when or whether the story dramatizes them. What this is not: This block does not decide when the reader learns these things. It does not decide how characters change over the story. It establishes the truth at the start of TLTT that the arc departs from. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ *Thematic Argumentation Design* Function: Establish what propositions the story is testing, what evidence must be deposited for the reader to arrive at those propositions independently, what counterarguments must be given honest weight, and whether the proposition is being demonstrated or merely illustrated. Mindset: Philosopher and rhetorician. The question being asked is: what is the story arguing, and would an honest skeptic find the argument convincing based on what they are shown? The test is whether the counterargument gets its due. Skills: Argument construction. Counterargument identification. The ability to distinguish evidence from assertion. Understanding of the difference between a story that argues and a story that preaches. Output: Thematic propositions and evidence plans. Not "harmony can scale" as an assertion but "the reader should be shown X, Y, and Z, from which they should be able to construct the conclusion that harmony can scale, having felt the genuine pull of the counterargument that it cannot." What this is not: This block does not design specific scenes. It establishes what scenes must accomplish in the aggregate. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ *Macro Narrative Architecture* Function: Decide the information structure of the whole story. What does the reader know and when? What is withheld and until which chapter? What gaps operate at the story level — where the reader and characters share the same wrong model until a revelation corrects both simultaneously? What gaps operate at the scene level — where the reader perceives more than the character through FID? Mindset: Strategic architect. The question being asked is: given what is canonically true and given the thematic argument I am making, what is the optimal sequence of revelation that produces the reader's experience of the whole story? Skills: Dramatic structure at the macro level. Information management across a long work. Long-form pacing. The ability to distinguish a Type A gap (reader and character share wrong model, corrected by revelation) from a Type B gap (reader perceives more than character through limited POV). Output: Architectural decisions. "The Stagnation's true start date is withheld from reader and characters until Chapter 22." "Luna's actual psychology is legible to the reader through behavioral implication before the confession in Scene 22.12, but not named until that scene." These are governing decisions that constrain scene-level design. What this is not: This block does not design individual scenes. It sets the parameters within which scene design operates. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ *Scene-Level Experience Design* Function: For each scene, design how the structural truth is experienced through a specific POV character's epistemic limits. Specify the gap between the POV character's self-perception and the reader's perception, the gap between the POV character's perception of observed characters and the reader's perception of them, and the non-negotiable concrete anchors that must be present in the prose to produce those gaps without stating them. Mindset: Director and reader psychologist. The question being asked is: given what is canonically true, given the architectural decisions about what the reader knows by this point, and given this POV character's epistemic limits — what should the reader experience in this scene, and what specific physical and behavioral details make that experience happen? Skills: FID calibration. Gap design. Reader experience modeling. The ability to think simultaneously as the POV character and as the reader watching the POV character. Output: Design specifications. POVBelief, ReaderPerception, GapType, FIDAnchors, ReaderState. These are decisions about reader experience, not about world truth. What this is not: This block does not write prose. It specifies what prose must produce. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ *Scene Blueprint* Function: Establish the ordered sequence of events as experienced by the POV character in real time. No psychology, no reader state analysis, no craft notes. Just: what happens, in what order, from inside one consciousness. Mindset: Director blocking a scene. The question being asked is: in what sequence do the events of this scene unfold, and what does the POV character encounter first? Skills: Pacing. Sequencing. POV discipline — the ability to describe only what the POV character can experience in real time. Output: The Synopsis field content as it was meant to function. A clean ordered delivery sequence. What this is not: This block does not explain why things happen or what they mean. That lives in the experience design and the canonical records. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ *Prose Drafting* Function: Execute the designed experience in language. Inhabit the POV character's voice and consciousness, use FID to render their perception while leaving the gap open for the reader, and deploy the FIDAnchors specified in the experience design. Mindset: Author inhabiting a voice. The question being asked is not "what is true" or "what should the reader experience" — those are already decided. The question is: what specific words, in what specific rhythm, carrying what specific sensory texture, produce the designed experience for an actual reader? Skills: Sentence craft. Voice consistency. FID execution. Sensory grounding. Micro-scale pacing. The ability to trust the design and not over-explain. Output: Prose. What this is not: This block does not make structural decisions. If a structural decision is required during drafting — because the design turns out to be impossible or incorrect — work stops and returns to the experience design block or the canonical record block before continuing. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ *Continuity Verification* Function: Check that prose executes its design specification, that design specifications are consistent with the canonical records they were built against, and that the chronological scan of character and thread connections shows coherent arcs without drift. Mindset: Developmental editor. The question being asked is: does this actually do what it was designed to do, and is the design internally consistent with the world it was designed against? Skills: Critical distance from the material. Arc-level reading. The ability to use the story planner's scan function as an analytical tool rather than a reference tool. Output: Corrections at the design level or the prose level. Flagged inconsistencies. Structural revision directives. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ *Structural Revision* Function: When a canonical claim changes — as the Stagnation date changed — trace all downstream content that was built against the prior claim, identify what must be revised, and update both the canonical record and the affected design specifications. Mindset: Structural engineer reviewing load-bearing changes. The question being asked is: what depended on the thing that changed? Skills: Dependency tracking. Consistency checking across a distributed system. The discipline to complete the revision rather than updating only the most visible downstream content. Output: Updated canonical assertions, flagged downstream design specifications, revision notes documenting what changed and why. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ *Publication and Reader Integration* Function: Publish polished chapters serially and use reader responses as calibration data about whether designs produced their intended effects in actual readers. Mindset: Community member and calibration listener. The question being asked is: did the design produce what it was intended to produce in a naive reader? Skills: Feedback interpretation. The ability to distinguish a reader's preference from a structural signal. Understanding of which reader responses indicate design failure versus reader variation. Output: Calibration data for later experience design decisions. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Whether Every Category Speaks to One Purpose No. The work blocks and the data categories do not map one-to-one, and forcing them to would be incorrect. A single work block — Character Psychology Construction — produces content that goes into multiple data containers: Track A for current psychology, Track B for formative history, and the architectural decision field for how that psychology is revealed. A single data container — the PlotPoint — serves multiple work blocks: it receives Scene Blueprint content from the Scene Blueprint block, Experience Design content from the Experience Design block, and Obligation content from the Classification block. What maps one-to-one is *assertion type to field*. Each field holds assertions of exactly one type. This is the correct unit of precision. The work block produces a range of assertion types. The field stores one. The data model's job is to enforce the one-to-one relationship at the field level. The work blocks are the human cognitive processes that generate the content. They do not need to be one-to-one with fields — they need to produce outputs that are correctly typed so they can be routed to the correct fields. The story planner's coherence comes from field-level assertion type homogeneity, not from work-block-to-container correspondence. You said: I feel like I am achieving more and more clarity. Show more Apr 20 Claude responded: The Core Diagnosis Is Correct Synthesized orthogonal axes and diagnosed hybrid note pollution problem Synthesized orthogonal axes and diagnosed hybrid note pollution problem The Core Diagnosis Is Correct You have independently arrived at the right architectural insight: the story planner is two orthogonal systems occupying the same containers, and the mixing is what produces all the problems you've described. *Axis 1 — Content type.* What kind of assertion is this? World mechanic, historical fact, character psychology, architectural decision, thematic proposition, obligation, bibliographic reference, craft observation. *Axis 2 — Stability state.* How confident are you in this content, and what work does it still require? Every piece of content has both a position on the content axis and a position on the stability axis. The current system only tracks content type implicitly (by which container it was placed in) and tracks stability barely at all (NeedsFurtherAnalysis on some notes only). Everything else is working memory and convention. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Unified Note Entity Yes. Making everything a Note instance with typed metadata is architecturally correct and not overengineering. The reason is precisely what you identified: the project management dimension — stability state, work directives, confidence level — is *orthogonal to content type* and therefore must be trackable independently of it. If stability state is only expressible by which field content sits in, it cannot be tracked orthogonally. If it is a property of the Note entity itself, it can be filtered, scanned, and managed independently of what the note contains. This means the current text fields on PlotPointCharacter, PlotPointTheme, PlotPointCodexEntry, and PlotPointStoryThread all become Note instances assigned to those connections, with the same metadata structure as notes on standalone entities. The connection is still the organizational unit. The note is the content unit. The metadata lives on the note. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Track Declares the Content Type Yes, this is correct. A note in the World Mechanics track of a Codex entry is, by virtue of its track placement, a world mechanics canonical claim. The track is the assertion type declaration. You do not need a separate metadata field for assertion type — track placement does that work. What the track does *not* declare is stability. A note in the World Mechanics track can be a stable canonical claim or a provisional hypothesis. The stability state must be carried separately because it is orthogonal to content type. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Three-State Model Is Correct, With Precise Naming Two states are insufficient because they conflate two different conditions that require different responses. *Unset* — captured but not yet evaluated. This note exists. Its content has not been scrutinized for correctness, completeness, or consistency with adjacent content. It should not be used as a design axiom. This is the default state for all raw capture. *Flagged* — evaluated and found to require active work. This is NeedsFurtherAnalysis renamed. The crucial design implication is what you identified: *this state must be accompanied by a separate obligation note specifying what the work is.* The Flagged state on a canonical claim note means "this claim has an open question" — but the question itself is not embedded in the claim note. It exists as a separate Obligation note linked to or adjacent to the flagged note. This is the solution to the hybrid note pollution problem. *Verified* — evaluated and confirmed stable within current structural context. Not permanent. Revisable when upstream content changes. But stable enough to be used as a design axiom by downstream work. The state transition model is: raw capture creates Unset notes. Review either Verifies them or Flags them. Flagged notes get companion Obligation notes. Discharged obligations allow Flagged notes to transition to Verified or to be revised and then Verified. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Hybrid Note Solution Is Splitting, Not Metadata The earth pony magic example illustrates this precisely. The current state is: Note A (World Mechanics track, Earth Pony Magic entry): "Earth pony magic scales with pride in the crop." — looks Verified, is actually superseded. Note B (somewhere in a synopsis field): "Earth pony magic works on both charitostatics and thymodynamics." — is actually the current canonical claim, stored in the wrong container. Architectural intention (working memory only): "Fleur's 'pride in the crop' perception is the POV-accessible version. The reader and characters hold this until a certain chapter. The real mechanic is withheld as a Type A gap." — has no container at all. The correct state after migration is: Note A (World Mechanics track, Earth Pony Magic entry, state: *Superseded/Archived*): "Earth pony magic scales with pride in the crop." — preserved as the record of a prior claim, marked as replaced by Note C. Note C (World Mechanics track, Earth Pony Magic entry, state: *Verified*): "Earth pony magic operates through charitostatics and thymodynamics." — the current canonical claim. Note D (Architectural Decision track, Earth Pony Magic entry, state: *Unset*): "The charitostatic/thymodynamic truth is withheld from characters and reader as a Type A gap. Fleur's 'pride in the crop' framing is the character-accessible version that operates as the functional truth until [chapter X]. This generates a reader-character shared wrong model to be corrected by revelation." — the macro narrative architecture decision, in its correct container. This requires a fourth state: *Superseded*. This is distinct from Flagged because it is not a work directive. It is a record that this claim existed and was replaced. Superseded notes should be visually distinct and non-prominent but preserved, because they are the revision history. When you do a structural revision, the prior claim gets Superseded rather than deleted, and the revision note on the new claim references the superseded one. The four states are therefore: *Unset, Flagged, Verified, Superseded.* ------------------------------------------------------------------------ One Notes-to-Self Track Per Entity, Not Per Sub-Track Having a notes-to-self section within each track would produce exactly the mixing problem it is trying to solve at a smaller scale. If the World Mechanics track contains both canonical claims and notes-to-self about those claims, the track is no longer homogeneous in assertion type. The correct architecture is one *Project Notes* track per entity, sitting alongside the content tracks. This track holds all project management content for that entity: open questions, work directives, cross-reference reminders, revision history summaries, and meta- observations about the entity's current state of development. None of this content is story content. It is all author-to-author communication across time. The Project Notes track on the Earth Pony Magic entry would currently contain: "Canonical claim revised from pride-based to charitostatic/ thymodynamic. Prior claim preserved as Superseded. Architectural decision for Type A gap pending design — see Note D. Downstream content that referenced pride-based version: [list of affected plot points and character notes]." ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Whether This Is Overengineering It is not overengineering given the specific conditions of this project. The argument is not abstract — it follows directly from the three conditions that distinguish your situation from a simpler one. *Condition 1: Scale and duration.* 108 Stage 0 obligations, an expanding world with active structural revisions, a multi-year project worked in non-continuous sessions with significant working memory loss between them. At this scale, the cost of not having the system is not inconvenience — it is the earth pony magic problem recurring across hundreds of notes. The superseded claim sitting next to the canonical claim with no distinction is not a minor inconsistency. It is an actively misleading structural record that will produce incorrect downstream design. *Condition 2: The serial publication constraint.* Published chapters cannot be silently revised. The stability state tracking is not just for your working memory. It is the mechanism that prevents an Unset note from being used as the basis for prose that gets published before the claim is Verified. An explicit Verified state is the gate between design and drafting. *Condition 3: You are the sole author and the developmental editor simultaneously.* A professional novelist's editor catches the earth pony magic problem during manuscript review. You need a system that catches it during design, because there is no manuscript review phase with an external reader. The four-state model with Superseded as a revision record is the closest available substitute for an editorial revision history. What would be overengineering: implementing the full architecture across all 108 Stage 0 obligations before doing any further expansion work. The system should be implemented for new content immediately and applied to existing content progressively, starting with the most load-bearing canonical claims that other design work depends on. The earth pony magic entry warrants immediate migration. A note about a minor Tzinacatl cultural detail that has no downstream dependencies can wait. The correct scope for now is: implement the data model changes (unified Note entity, four states, track structure with Project Notes track), apply immediately to all new content, and migrate existing content in priority order based on what is most likely to be used as a design axiom in the near term. Show more Apr 20 Claude responded: The Precise Taxonomy The Precise Taxonomy Rather than first/second/third order, which implies a linear hierarchy, the correct distinction is by *what the text is made of* and *what it is used for*. These are two independent axes. Let me name each category precisely. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Category 1: Story Content Text that describes, asserts, or specifies something that exists or occurs within the story's world or the reader's experience of it. It is made of claims about the story. Its use is to be true within the story's frame. Story Content subdivides into four kinds: *World truth* — assertions about what is invariantly real in the story's world, independent of any character's knowledge. "Earth pony magic operates through charitostatics and thymodynamics." This is what it is regardless of who knows it or when the reader learns it. *Historical record* — assertions about what objectively happened, in sequence, causally connected. "The Griffonian Republic was founded in 978. Chrysalis's economic consolidation began with the red love export in 979." Events stated as facts. *Psychological architecture* — assertions about who a character is and why, independent of what they demonstrate in any given scene. "Twilight's core wound is dependency on external authority for conscience." True regardless of when or whether it is dramatized. *Thematic proposition* — assertions about what argument the story is making and what evidence must be deposited. "The reader must feel the genuine pull of the counterargument that harmony cannot scale before the confirming evidence arrives." This is story content because it is about what the story does, not about what the author does next. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Category 2: Narrative Architecture Text that describes how Story Content is structured in time for the reader. It is made of decisions about information flow. Its use is to govern when and how world truths, historical facts, and character psychologies become accessible to the reader and to the POV characters. Narrative Architecture subdivides into two kinds: *Macro architecture* — governs information across multiple chapters or the whole story. "The Stagnation's true start date is withheld from characters and reader until Chapter 22. Characters and reader share the 1000-year wrong model together until Celestia's revelation corrects both." This is not a world truth — the world truth is 930 ALB. This is a decision about when that truth enters the reading experience. *Scene architecture* — governs information within a single scene. "AJ believes she has found her proper place. The reader perceives she has found her hiding place. The FID should render her contentment as genuine. The radio on her back is the non-negotiable physical anchor." This is the experience design for one scene. It is not world truth and not prose. The reason Narrative Architecture is not Story Content is that it could change without the world being wrong. If you decided to reveal the Stagnation date earlier, the world truth would remain 930 ALB. The architecture would change. These are independent. The reason Narrative Architecture is not prose is that it specifies what prose must produce without being prose itself. "The reader should feel the contentment as real" is not the sentence that produces that feeling. It is the specification for the sentence. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Category 3: Prose Text that is the story — language that a reader encounters as the narrative. It is made of words performing voice, sensory experience, rhythm, and FID. Its use is to be read. Prose subdivides into two kinds: *Committed prose* — final or near-final language intended to appear in the published work. Lives in the VerbatimText field. *Prose fragments* — captured phrases, lines, or passages produced during raw capture that may become committed prose. These are currently scattered in Synopsis fields. They are not Scene Architecture — they are linguistic candidates. They belong in a dedicated fragment container, not mixed with delivery sequences. The reason prose is separate from Narrative Architecture is that the same architecture can be executed by many different prose choices. "Render her contentment as genuine" can be executed by an infinite number of sentences. The architecture is the specification. The prose is one execution of it. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Category 4: Project Management Text Text that is made of the author's instructions to themselves across time. Its use is to direct future work, record decisions, track obligations, and manage the project's state. It has no story content. It is not readable as part of the story or the story's design. It is correspondence from the author at time T to the author at time T+N. Project Management Text subdivides into four kinds: *Obligation* — specifies work that must be done before a piece of content can be treated as Verified. "Research how medieval tally sticks functioned before finalizing Tally Mark's Track B." The obligation is not the content. It is the reason the content is Flagged. *Revision directive* — records that a canonical claim has changed and specifies what downstream content must be reviewed. "Stagnation start date revised from 0 ALB to 930 ALB. Review: Celestia Track A, all references to 1000-year duration in theme payloads and plot point notes." This is not story content and not architecture. It is a change management record. *Cross-reference* — records a dependency or connection that is not formally captured in the data model. "This scene's gap depends on the reader having processed the Stalliongrad reports in 12.12. Verify 12.12 is placed correctly in chapter order." This directs future attention without asserting anything about the story. *Meta-observation* — records the author's current understanding of a design problem without resolving it. "I am not sure whether Mali's backstory should be revealed through her POV or through Fleur's perception. Hold this question." This is not a hypothesis about the world. It is a flag on an unresolved architectural decision. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Why Superseded Should Not Be a Note State Your instinct is correct. The Superseded state would require a systems engineer reading the World Mechanics track to context-switch into project manager mode to understand why a superseded note is present. The cognitive load is real and the mixing is real. The correct architecture is: when a canonical claim is revised, the old claim is not retained in the World Mechanics track. It is moved to the *Project Notes track* of the same entity as a Revision Directive. "Prior claim: earth pony magic scales with pride in the crop. Superseded by charitostatic/thymodynamic model after griffonian history expansion. Archived here for reference." The World Mechanics track then contains only the current canonical claim, Verified. The Project Notes track contains the revision history. A systems engineer reading the World Mechanics track sees only current claims. A project manager reading the Project Notes track sees the revision history. No cognitive mode mixing. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Flag Field Architecture Your instinct about the flag and its reason is also correct. Separating the flag from the reason defeats the purpose. When you return to a Flagged note after three weeks, the note says "earth pony magic scales with pride in the crop" and is colored, but the reason for the flag is gone. You have to reconstruct why it was flagged from working memory. The solution is a *FlagNote* field on the Note entity, separate from the content field. The content field holds Story Content or Narrative Architecture. The FlagNote field holds Project Management Text — specifically the obligation or meta-observation that explains why this note is Flagged. A note therefore has: *Content* — the story design assertion. Displayed to a systems engineer. *FlagNote* — the obligation or reason for flagging. Displayed only when the note is Flagged. Empty when Verified or Unset. *State* — Unset, Flagged, Verified. The FlagNote is part of the note entity, not a separate note. It is directly attached to the claim it qualifies. When you read a Flagged claim, you immediately see why it is flagged without navigating to a separate entity. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Visual Separation Within a Track Yes, they should be visually separated, but by state rather than by arbitrary positioning. The correct visual model is: *Verified notes* — displayed first, full prominence. These are the canonical truths the track is for. They are what a systems engineer reads. *Unset notes* — displayed second, reduced prominence. These are candidates. They are visible but marked as provisional. *Flagged notes* — displayed with a distinct marker and the FlagNote visible. They are neither hidden nor prominent. They signal active work without contaminating the clean read of Verified content. A reader doing story design reads primarily the Verified notes. A reader doing project management reads the Flagged notes. A reader doing review reads all three in their visual hierarchy. The visual separation enforces the cognitive mode rather than requiring the author to remember to switch modes. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Whether the Second Order Should Split Yes. The split you identified between Narrative Architecture and Scene Architecture is not a distinction within a single order — it is a distinction between two genuinely different cognitive modes and two different downstream uses. *World truths, historical record, psychological architecture, and thematic proposition* are Story Content. The cognitive mode for producing them is analytical and systems-oriented. Their downstream use is to be true — to be the axioms against which everything else is calibrated. *Macro Narrative Architecture* is a decision layer above Story Content. The cognitive mode is strategic and structural — deciding when truth enters the reading experience. Its downstream use is to constrain Scene Architecture. *Scene Architecture* (POVBelief, ReaderPerception, GapType, FIDAnchors) is a design layer above Macro Architecture. The cognitive mode is rhetorical and psychological — deciding how a specific scene produces a specific experience. Its downstream use is to constrain prose. *Prose* is the execution layer. The cognitive mode is linguistic and rhythmic. These are four distinct orders in terms of cognitive mode and dependency. Each depends on the one above it being locally stable. Scene Architecture cannot be correctly designed without Macro Architecture being decided for the relevant information. Macro Architecture cannot be correctly decided without Story Content being canonically established. Prose cannot be correctly drafted without Scene Architecture being specified. The reason not to call them first/second/third/fourth order is that the word "order" implies they are versions of the same thing at different levels of abstraction. They are not. They are genuinely different kinds of text produced by genuinely different cognitive modes for genuinely different purposes. The names should reflect that difference: Story Content, Macro Architecture, Scene Architecture, Prose. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ What Notes to Self Actually Means From your sampled data, every instance of what functions as a note to self falls into one of the four Project Management Text categories. There is no unified thing called "notes to self." The phrase is an umbrella that covers at least four different things: From your sample, the Chapter Note "Need to determine the order of Astler, Sickleclaw and Rosewing that makes the best progression and message" is a *Meta-observation* — an unresolved architectural decision flagged for future work. The note "Fleur has an inkling that Mali worked at a parloir / The letter describes hyper empathy" mixed into a PlotPointCharacter payload is partly a *Psychological architecture claim* about Mali and partly a *Cross-reference* noting that Fleur's perception of Mali is relevant to a future scene. The note "Need to include an allegory how Celestia did not encourage calculators in school for math" is an *Obligation* — a directive to incorporate a specific element. The note "Transition to deep third with a sensory hook when emotional stakes rise" from Scene 33.2 is a *Craft observation* — a decision about prose technique that belongs in Scene Architecture or a general craft notes track. The note appended to a flagged world truth with a question is a *FlagNote* — the reason a canonical claim is not yet Verified. None of these are the same thing. The phrase "notes to self" conceals this. The correct response is to eliminate the category entirely and replace it with the four Project Management Text types, each going to its correct location: obligations and meta-observations as FlagNotes on the content they qualify, revision directives in the Project Notes track, cross-references in the Project Notes track, craft observations in a Scene Architecture track or general prose notes section. The fatally general quality of "notes to self" is not a problem with the concept — the problem is that the concept does not exist as a unified thing. It is a label applied to residual text that had no home. Once the homes exist with precise assertion types, the residual disappears because everything has a correct location. The Parloirs note comparing Aquileian aesthetic with Griffonian solidarity foundation, the Clothing note comparing Griffonian, Equestrian, and Manehattan clothing cultures, and the Establishing the Equestrian Republic Note 2 comparing Chrysalis's extraction model against the Republic's charitostatic model — these are analytical comparisons that synthesize multiple world truths into a single relational claim. They are more than individual world truths. They are structural insights about how parts of the world relate to each other, which makes them load-bearing for both thematic argumentation and macro narrative architecture. A Synthesis note is not a new fundamental category — it is a world truth that takes multiple other world truths as premises. But it needs to be recognized as a distinct sub-type because its verification requires all its premise truths to be Verified first. A Synthesis note cannot be Verified if any of its premises are still Unset. *Dual-Register Content.* Scene 28.3's Chrysalis character note contains: "Eros looks at Chrysalis and sees an apex predator. Gabriella looks at Chrysalis and sees The Girl She Hugged. A narcissist will always choose the person who fears them over the person who pities them. Chrysalis betrays Gabriella because she wants to erase the version of herself that needed a hug." This is simultaneously valid as Psychological Architecture for Chrysalis's Track A and as Scene Architecture for the ObservedReaderPerception in this specific scene. The same analytical insight does two different jobs at two different levels of the system. This is not a new category — it is a content splitting problem. The Track A version states the psychological truth in the abstract. The Scene Architecture version states how that truth is deployed and perceived in this specific scene. Both versions need to exist. The current note contains both in undifferentiated form and should be split on migration. You said: What other metadata could be useful on notes and why? The author is the only reviewer, so the word should reflect internal deliberate review rather than external confirmation. *Confirmed* is slightly more accurate — it implies the author has actively examined this note and decided it stands. It also works semantically across all note types: a world truth is confirmed, a psychological architecture claim is confirmed, an obligation is confirmed as resolved. *Stable* is precise about what the state actually means — stable within current structural context, revisable if upstream content changes — but it sounds too technical for a UI label. *Established* works well for content notes but feels awkward for obligation notes. The recommendation is *Confirmed*, with the understanding that for obligation notes in the Project Notes track, Confirmed means discharged rather than validated. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Mandatory Fields — Confirmed The note entity needs: Track placement (declaration by container), State (Unset / Flagged / Confirmed), FlagReason (text field, visible only when Flagged, attached directly to the note rather than as a separate entity), WorldDate (optional, relevant to Historical Record track notes and timeline view). These are not up for debate. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Source Material Field Keep it, but understand its precise value so it doesn't become noise. Its value is specifically on *canonical constraints* — world truths, historical record notes, and psychological architecture notes that originate in FiM, EaW, or P&K canon. A world truth derived from canon is a harder constraint than one you invented. If you later design something that appears to contradict it, the source material tag tells you whether you are subverting a hard canon constraint (which requires deliberate in-universe justification) or simply revising your own design choice (which is free). Concretely: a note saying "Cutie marks appear when a pony discovers their special talent" tagged as FiM-derived is a different kind of constraint than "Earth pony magic scales with charitostatics," which is your invention. Both are world truths. But when a downstream scene design seems to pressure one of them, the source tag tells you whether pressure means justification work or simple revision. The source material field has zero value on project management notes, bibliographic notes, and prose fragments. It is moderately valuable on content notes where provenance matters. Mark it as optional and only populate it when the note's origin is meaningfully constraining. This matches the existing implementation as optional and unused — not wrong, just waiting for the right data sanitation pass. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ High-Value Candidate: Story Scope The multi-story architecture discussion established that TLTT, prequels, and sequels share layers 1, 2, and 3 but have distinct layer 4 deployments. A *StoryScope* tag on notes would distinguish: Content that is true across all stories — most world truths, most historical record, foundational psychological architecture. These are shared and should be visible when designing any story. Content that is specific to one story's layer 4 deployment — a macro architecture note about when a truth is revealed in TLTT is irrelevant when designing a prequel. Scene architecture notes are always story- specific. The practical value is that as prequel design begins, the note view for any entity can filter to show only content relevant to the active story, rather than displaying TLTT-specific architectural decisions alongside cross-story world truths. However, this is not immediately useful. You are deep in Stage 0 for TLTT and have not begun prequel design. The StoryScope field would sit unused for a long time. The correct decision is: design the data model to accommodate it (add the field as nullable), do not populate it during the current stage, and revisit when prequel design begins. It costs nothing to have the field exist unpopulated. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Moderate-Value Candidate: POV Tag on Scene Architecture Notes Scene architecture notes in a PlotPointCharacter payload are implicitly scoped to the scene's POV character, which is recorded on the PlotPoint itself. But if notes migrate to a unified Note entity attached to connections, the POV character is no longer implicit — it must be either inferred from context or explicitly tagged. A lightweight solution is a *POVCharacterId* reference on the Note entity, nullable, relevant only to notes in scene architecture tracks. When populated, the dashboard can show you all scene architecture notes for AJ across every scene she appears in, which is the character-level scan applied specifically to experience design rather than arc tracking. The value is real but deferred. Scene architecture work has not begun. The field is low-cost to add now and high-value when that work starts. Same recommendation as StoryScope: add as nullable, leave unpopulated. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Low-Value Candidates Examined *Numeric confidence score.* The three-state model covers confidence adequately. Unset is uncertain, Flagged is actively problematic, Confirmed is stable. Adding a 1-5 scale would require calibration decisions on hundreds of notes that would inevitably be inconsistent and would not produce meaningfully different filtering behavior than the three states already enable. *Priority or urgency on obligation notes.* The Flagged state already signals active work needed. Prioritization among flagged notes is a working-session decision that depends on what else is in progress, which changes constantly. A static priority tag set during capture would be stale within days. Better handled by working-session judgment. *Export flag.* The user exports curated content to AI sessions for analysis. But the export function can serve this better by filtering on state (export only Confirmed notes from relevant tracks) and entity scope (export only entities related to the current design question) rather than requiring per-note tagging. A per-note export flag would require manual maintenance across hundreds of notes with no clear maintenance trigger. *Revision history.* A full audit trail of note content changes would be valuable in a professional collaborative setting. For a solo project, the FlagReason field captures the reason for revision, and the Confirmed state records that the revision was made. Full content versioning is infrastructure the project does not need. *Word count or length metric.* Useful for token management when exporting to AI sessions, but this is a display-layer computation, not stored metadata. The NoteViewModel can compute and display this on demand without storing it. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The NoteViewModel UI Priorities Given the mandatory fields and the confirmed optional additions, the NoteViewModel should make the following design decisions worthwhile: Within each track, the visual separation should be Confirmed notes at full prominence first, Unset notes second at reduced visual weight, Flagged notes third with the FlagReason inline and a distinct visual treatment. This ordering ensures a systems engineer reading a track gets the canonical truths immediately without scrolling past uncertain content. The FlagReason field should appear inline beneath the note content when the state is Flagged, and collapse to invisible when Confirmed or Unset. It should be editable in place so that during a review session, you can both change the state and record the reason in a single interaction. WorldDate should display as a compact field, visible only for notes in the Historical Record track, and should feed directly into the timeline view. Notes without a WorldDate in the Historical Record track should be visually marked as incomplete — this creates an organic pressure to date historical claims without requiring enforcement. The state transition should be a single interaction — a segmented control or a right-click menu — rather than a form submission. The cost of state changes must be low enough that keeping the state current is not a burden. If setting a note to Confirmed requires more than two interactions, it will not get set consistently. A per-entity note count by state, visible in the entity list view, gives the project management dashboard without requiring a separate screen. Seeing that the Earth Pony Magic codex entry has 3 Confirmed, 4 Unset, and 2 Flagged notes in its World Mechanics track is actionable information that motivates targeted review sessions. I was planning on also using F1-F12 hotkeys for assigning note tracks Show more Apr 20 Claude responded: Deletion of Fulfilled Obligations Deletion of Fulfilled Obligations Yes, deletion is correct for pure obligations that have been discharged with no lasting significance. An obligation is a task, not content. Once the task is done, the obligation note has no downstream value. Keeping it inflates the note count, adds visual noise to the project management track, and creates no benefit. The judgment call is whether the discharged obligation produced content that should be documented elsewhere. If you researched medieval tally sticks and the answer became a Confirmed Historical Record note on Tally Mark's Track B, the obligation is gone and the output is preserved correctly. If the research led to the conclusion that no change was needed — the original note was already accurate — deletion with no trace is also correct. The only case for preservation is when the *process of investigation* was itself significant to the story's development — a paradigm shift like the Stagnation of Harmony date revision, where understanding why the change was made is useful to future you. That kind of event is a Meta-Observation on the entity, as you correctly identified. It is not an obligation note that gets promoted; it is a new note of a different type written to record the evolution. Meta-Observations do not need a Confirmed state because they are inherently provisional and reflective rather than load-bearing. A Meta- Observation cannot be wrong in the way a world truth can be wrong. It can become outdated — if a further revision supersedes the one being documented — in which case it either gets updated or deleted. The appropriate state for Meta-Observations is either Unset (just written, not reviewed for clarity or completeness) or a soft confirmation that means "I've read this back and it accurately records my current thinking." Given that Meta-Observations are not axioms for downstream work, a two-state model of Unset and Reviewed works better than Unset/ Confirmed, to prevent conflating authorial reflection with canonical stability. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ On the Term Meta-Observation The term is analytically precise but not intuitive. Better options: *Author's Log* — captures the journal-like, time-indexed nature of these notes. An author's log entry records what you were thinking at a specific moment in the project's development. Slightly informal but immediately legible. *Project Journal* — similar, perhaps too broad. *Design Reflection* — captures that these notes are about the design process itself, not the design output. Clear and professional but slightly sterile. *Working Note* — simple, implies the author at work rather than the work itself. Risk of being too generic. *Development Note* — used in software to mean a note about the development process rather than the artifact being developed. Directly analogous to your use case. The recommendation is *Development Note*. It is distinct from content notes, implies the author's perspective on the project's development, and does not require explanation to understand what it holds. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Saving Canon Truths The changeling hive mind example is the correct heuristic stated precisely: *save canon truths that have been hallucinated, not all canon truths*. The general principle is that LLMs hallucinate toward the most statistically common version of a concept. Changelings in popular culture and other fanfiction frequently have hive minds. The LLM's prior is hive mind until corrected. The note "changelings do NOT have a hive mind" is not a world truth in the usual sense — it is a *hallucination refutation*, a note whose purpose is to override an LLM prior rather than to assert something you invented. This is a distinct sub-type of World Truth note with a specific purpose in the export pipeline. A hallucination refutation note is always phrased as a negation or explicit correction: "NOT X" or "contrary to common depictions, X is false in this world." Its presence signals to the export parser that this note is high-priority for AI sessions and should appear early in context rather than embedded in a long block of positive assertions. The practical question of whether to store general canon truths like "cutie marks appear when a pony discovers their special talent" depends on whether you need the LLM to reason about them. If the AI session involves something where cutie mark mechanics are relevant and the LLM needs the correct mechanics to give useful analysis, the note earns its export slot. If the session is about Chrysalis's financial schemes, the note adds tokens without value. This is context-sensitivity — the note exists in the system and gets included or excluded based on the session's scope, not stored or omitted based on global importance. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Export Strategy The previous all-in export is analytically correct for Gemini sessions and analytically incorrect for Claude sessions, for the reasons you identified. But "all confirmed notes" versus "nothing" is still not the right frame. The correct frame is *export by session purpose*, and each session purpose has a natural scope. *Gemini open-ended sessions:* Export Confirmed and Unset content notes (world truths, historical record, psychological architecture) for all entities relevant to the general area being explored. Exclude project management tracks entirely — Development Notes, obligations, revision directives — because these add tokens without informing world-building analysis. Exclude scene architecture notes because the session is not designing scenes. Exclude prose fragments. Include bibliographic notes only if the session specifically involves real-world analogue analysis. *Claude focused sessions:* Export only entities and tracks directly relevant to the specific question. If the session is about Twilight's experience in Scene 25.10, export: Twilight's Track A and Track B, the relevant codex entries' World Mechanics tracks, the scene's existing notes, and any relevant macro architecture notes on the entities involved. Exclude everything else. The hallucination refutation notes for entities in scope should always be included regardless of session type. *What never exports:* Development Notes, FlagReasons (these are internal project state), obligations, and prose fragments. Prose fragments are your work and should not be fed back to an LLM. *What conditionally exports based on session type:* Bibliographic notes only for analogue analysis sessions. Thematic proposition notes only for sessions involving argument structure review. Social commentary and allegory notes fall in this category — high value for sessions specifically about whether a theme is landing correctly, low value for world mechanics expansion sessions. The UI implication is a session type selector that pre-populates which tracks to include, with manual override. Three presets cover the common cases: World Expansion (all content notes, no project management, no architecture), Architecture Review (confirmed content notes plus macro architecture notes, no project management), Scene Design (confirmed content for in-scope entities, plus existing scene architecture notes for reference). These presets are starting points — the degree-of- separation rule you mentioned governs which entities to include within any preset. *The degree-of-separation rule* is the right approach for entity scoping. For a focused session, include the primary entity at 0 degrees, all entities directly connected to it at 1 degree, and optionally entities connected to those at 2 degrees. More than 2 degrees produces diminishing returns because the content becomes progressively less relevant to the session's focus. The connection graph already exists via the PlotPoint connection entities — degree of separation can be computed from it. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Macro Architecture Placement and Scope You are identifying a genuine ambiguity that needs resolution. Let me state what Macro Architecture actually is precisely enough to resolve it. Macro Architecture is an assertion about *information control decisions* — what the reader knows, when they know it, what is withheld from them, and through which story they first encounter it. It is the author deciding how to deploy story content across reading time. This means Macro Architecture is inherently *project-scoped, not story- scoped*. The decision "Aquileia's pre-Discret feudal history is touched in TLTT through Minette's implied backstory but fully dramatized only in her prequel" is a Macro Architecture note that governs both TLTT and the prequel simultaneously. Scoping it to one story would misrepresent its nature — it is a decision about the relationship between two stories. The Macro Architecture track on an entity therefore holds notes in two registers: *Within-story architecture* — "The Stagnation's true start date is withheld from characters and reader until Chapter 22 of TLTT." This governs one story and could be labeled TLTT-specific, but since it is a decision about information control rather than a world truth, it still belongs in the Macro Architecture track rather than a content track. *Cross-story architecture* — "Aquileia's house pony history is seeded in TLTT through Celestia's reference to 925 ALB abuses, and fully dramatized in the Minette/Reni prequel. The TLTT treatment should produce weight without full exposition. The prequel is where the reader gains the full structural understanding that retroactively confirms the weight they felt in TLTT." This is a cross-story scope note. It governs what TLTT plants and what the prequel pays off. Both belong in the Macro Architecture track of the Aquileia entity. The StoryScope tag previously discussed as optional becomes meaningful here — tagging a Macro Architecture note as TLTT-specific versus project-wide lets the export pipeline include cross-story notes for all session types while filtering TLTT-specific architecture notes only for TLTT scene design sessions. The work block that uses cross-story Macro Architecture notes is the *Multi-Story Dependency Review* — the periodic check that what TLTT implies about a character or entity is consistent with what the prequel is designed to dramatize. This is not a separate work block in the taxonomy but a specific mode of the Continuity Verification block that operates across stories rather than within one. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ F1-F12 Hotkeys for Track Assignment This is correct and worth doing carefully. The hotkeys work best if they are assigned to the tracks that are most frequently used during the active work phase, not to all tracks globally. During the current Stage 0 and early Stage 1 phase, the most frequent track assignments are World Mechanics, Historical Record, Psychological Architecture, and Project Management. These four should have the most ergonomic hotkeys — F1 through F4, or whatever sits under natural finger positions. Scene Architecture and Macro Architecture tracks will be heavily used in later phases but rarely touched now. Assigning them to less ergonomic keys currently and reassigning during the transition to Stage 2 work is a reasonable approach, or they can occupy F5-F8 as secondary-use positions. The state transitions — Unset, Flagged, Confirmed — should also have hotkeys, ideally distinct from the track assignment keys. These are the most frequent single-note operations and should be executable without moving to a mouse. The FlagReason field should auto-focus when the Flagged state is applied via hotkey, so the reason gets entered immediately in the same gesture. You said: We've singled out the third person limited stuff to be explicit designs. We've singled out the third person limited stuff to be explicit designs. Is there anything else essential which should be designed? What fundamentally makes the third person limited related specific fields so special that they get dedicated forms in the application, as opposed to other stuff, and what other stuff could be elevated? But also don't over engineer. Only things that are powerful and not diminishing returns should be pointed out, and please explain candidates that are considered but not elevated. Also explain why the things we've selected are elevated Apr 21 Claude responded: The Principle Behind Elevation Identified elevation principles and evaluated thematic architecture as strongest candidate Identified elevation principles and evaluated thematic architecture as strongest candidate The Principle Behind Elevation A field deserves dedicated form status when it meets all four of these criteria simultaneously: *Distinct cognitive mode.* The content cannot be produced by the same thinking that produces adjacent content. It requires a deliberate mode- switch. If you can write it while staying in the same mental posture as the surrounding fields, it doesn't need its own form. *Diagnostic emptiness.* An empty field must mean something specific and actionable — not "this hasn't been written yet" but "a specific category of required work has not been done." The empty field is a work queue item with a known type. *Direct prose governance.* The field must directly constrain specific prose decisions in a way no other field does. It is the bridge between design and language. Without it, the writer either guesses or defaults to a predictable failure mode. *Specific failure mode prevention.* There must be an identifiable, recurring failure that this field's existence prevents. The failure mode must be one that happens without the field even when the writer has good intentions. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Why the Third Person Limited Fields Are Elevated They meet all four criteria precisely. *POVBelief* requires inhabiting a character's consciousness and stating their working theory — not what is true, not what the reader perceives, but what this character sincerely believes right now. This cannot be produced while thinking as a systems engineer documenting world truth or as a strategist planning revelation sequence. It requires a specific act of psychological inhabitation. *ReaderPerception* requires holding the reader's accumulated context and reasoning from their epistemic position rather than the author's omniscient one. This is a distinct cognitive act — modeling a consciousness that has read everything so far but knows nothing beyond it. Both fields are diagnostically empty in the most meaningful way: empty means Stage 2 has not been done, the scene cannot be written without omniscient default, and the gap will collapse. Both directly govern FID calibration — they specify what voice and what interiority to inhabit during drafting, and what the physical details must imply without stating. The failure mode they prevent is precisely the one your existing data demonstrates: omniscient narration dressed as third person limited, where structural truths leak through the POV character's thoughts as if they have omniscient access to their own irony. *GapType* is elevated for a different but complementary reason. It is a forced classification that prevents the author from treating all gaps as equivalent. The distinction between Ironic, Tragic, Closing, and Aligned has direct consequences for prose tone, FID voice, and what the reader should feel at scene's end. An unclassified gap defaults to Ironic when it might be Closing, producing prose that keeps the reader at a distance from a character who should be becoming legible to them. *FIDAnchors* is elevated because it is the only field that names specific concrete elements that must exist in the prose. Every other field specifies intent. FIDAnchors specifies required material — actual objects, behaviors, physical details that cannot be absent. Its emptiness means the prose has no non-negotiable anchors and the gap can evaporate during drafting through the natural pressure of the scene's emotional intensity. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Second Elevation: Thematic Evidence Architecture The *EvidenceDeposited* field on PlotPointTheme meets all four criteria with nearly equal force to the TPL fields and should be a dedicated form field. The cognitive mode required is rhetorical and argumentative — the author as a lawyer reviewing what evidence they have placed before a jury. This is genuinely different from the psychological inhabitation required for POVBelief or the systems engineering required for world truth. You are asking: what has the reader been shown in this scene that constitutes evidence for or against this proposition, and what will they likely conclude from it? This requires simultaneously holding the proposition, the scene's events, and a model of a naive reader's inference process. Diagnostic emptiness here is specific: an empty EvidenceDeposited field means the scene has a theme connection but the author has not verified that the scene is actually doing thematic work. The theme is assigned but undesigned. The failure mode this produces is the most common failure in thematically ambitious fiction — theme becomes decoration, then becomes preaching. The author knows what the theme is and stops there, trusting that having the theme in mind during drafting will be sufficient. It is not. Without specifying what the reader has been shown, the author defaults to having characters articulate the theme, which is precisely what converts proposition into sermon. The direct prose governance is: this field constrains what events must be present in the scene. If the EvidenceDeposited for the Honesty vs Poseurs theme in Scene 12.13 requires that the reader see Mali's challenge deflected rather than answered, then the scene must be written so that deflection is physically present and observable. If the field is empty, this specific requirement is invisible and may not make it into the prose. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Third Elevation: Chapter-Level Reader Arc The Chapter entity currently has free-form notes. A dedicated field for the *chapter's reader arc* — specifically, what question the reader enters the chapter with and what question or understanding they exit with — meets three of the four criteria and deserves a dedicated field, though it is slightly less essential than the previous two. The cognitive mode is structural and architectural at the chapter scope, distinct from scene-level experience design. You are asking what this chapter's unit of meaning is for the reader, which is different from asking what any individual scene produces. The diagnostic emptiness is meaningful: an empty field means the chapter has scenes but no governing reader arc. Individual scenes may be well- designed while the chapter fails to function as a unit because no one asked what question it answers. The failure mode it prevents is the chapter that resolves its plot questions but leaves the reader without a new overarching question to carry forward — what you previously identified as the difference between suspense (withheld event outcome) and resonance (resolved local question that reconfigures the overarching ones). Without a designated field, the chapter arc lives in free-form notes mixed with logistical planning, and is easily overlooked during scene design. Where it falls slightly short of full elevation: the chapter arc can sometimes be inferred from the accumulation of scene-level ReaderState fields and the thread payloads. The TPL fields and EvidenceDeposited govern content that cannot be inferred from other fields. The chapter arc is inferrable in principle, just practically difficult without the explicit field. This makes it a strong candidate but one step below mandatory. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Candidates Considered But Not Elevated *Cross-scene seed/payoff tracking.* When Scene 12.13 plants something that Chapter 22 must harvest, this relationship exists in FIDAnchors as a retroactive design note and in Macro Architecture notes on the relevant entities. The desire to give it its own form is understandable — it is important — but the relationship is already capturable in existing elevated fields without a new form. Adding a dedicated cross- scene reference field would require maintaining a separate reference graph that duplicates work already done in the connection entities and Macro Architecture notes. The marginal diagnostic value does not justify the maintenance cost. *Character arc trajectory per scene (ArcStatement).* This is already a field. Its elevation was implicit in the earlier framework discussion. It is the omniscient arc record that enables the chronological scan, and it meets the criteria because empty means the arc was not tracked at this moment. It is already elevated. *Sensory bridge specification for mid-scene POV transitions.* This is real design work that requires precise specification — the exact sentence or sensory anchor that carries the POV transition. But the sensory bridge occurs in a small number of scenes and its specification can live in FIDAnchors on the relevant scene without a dedicated field. Elevating it would create a dedicated form field used in perhaps a dozen scenes across the entire story. The diagnostic value of emptiness — signaling that a planned POV transition has no designed anchor — could instead be handled by a boolean on the PlotPoint indicating whether a mid-scene transition is planned, which would trigger display of a specific FIDAnchors sub-section. This is lower cost than a new field and captures the same diagnostic signal. *Counterfactual design for genuine counterarguments.* Every thematic scene should give the opposing proposition its honest weight. A dedicated field for "what is the strongest counterargument deposited in this scene" was considered as a companion to EvidenceDeposited. It does not meet the distinct cognitive mode criterion strongly enough — writing the counterargument well is part of the same rhetorical thinking as EvidenceDeposited, not a separate mode requiring its own cognitive switch. The counterargument can be incorporated into EvidenceDeposited as a required component of that field's content rather than as a separate form. *Revelation type classification for codex references.* A field categorizing whether a codex element's appearance in a scene is first reveal, accumulation, recontextualization, or payoff was considered as a companion to the existing UsageType enum. The UsageType enum already partially covers this. The additional classification would improve export filtering precision but does not meet the direct prose governance criterion — knowing whether a codex element is at its recontextualization moment does not constrain specific prose decisions in the way FIDAnchors does. The ReaderAccumulation field on PlotPointCodexEntry covers the reader experience design for codex appearances, which is the governance that matters.
