We are still using the same third person limited fields that were first designed before the whole framework was given new understanding. Please evaluate whether these fields and their naming are the best ones to use. Start again from first principles with the new foundation of the data taxonomy. The goal is to use first principles to determine where fields are best, with the new setup instead of what created when the foundation was the naive setup before. Don't be afraid to discard, but also don't be afraid to reaffirm. Apr 21 Claude responded: First Principles Re-evaluation Reconstructed scene architecture fields from foundational principles Reconstructed scene architecture fields from foundational principles First Principles Re-evaluation The previous field design was created when the primary insight was "third person limited requires gap design." The framework has since established a precise taxonomy with distinct assertion types, work blocks with distinct cognitive modes, and a Note entity system that carries state. The re-evaluation question is: given this full foundation, what is the correct complete set of elevated Scene Architecture fields, where do they live, and what are they named? The criteria for elevation remain the four established ones: distinct cognitive mode, diagnostic emptiness, direct prose governance, specific failure mode prevention. What changes is that we now also ask: does this field belong on the PlotPoint, on a connection entity, or as a structured Note within a track? And: does the name correctly identify what kind of assertion this is? ------------------------------------------------------------------------ What Changes: The Note Entity Reconsidered for These Fields The previous design treated POVBelief, ReaderPerception, GapType, and FIDAnchors as dedicated text fields on PlotPoint or PlotPointCharacter. Given the Note entity system, the question is whether these should become Notes in structured tracks rather than discrete fields. The answer splits by singularity. *Stakes, ReaderState, Synopsis, Outcome* on PlotPoint are singular — one per scene by definition. These remain discrete fields. Their emptiness is already diagnostic. State tracking adds little because their scope is the scene itself: if the scene exists, these either exist or signal a design gap. *POVBelief, POVReaderPerception, GapType, FIDAnchors* are also singular per scene — one POV character, one self-perception gap. They should be discrete fields on PlotPoint. However, they benefit from state tracking because Stage 2 work is a distinct phase. The resolution: these are a structured group of fields in a dedicated *Scene Architecture panel* on the PlotPoint form, displayed separately from the Stage 1 content fields. Visually and cognitively distinct. Not Note instances but fields with their own completion indicator — a panel-level status that reads Undesigned, In Progress, or Complete based on which fields are populated. *ObservedBelief, ObservedReaderPerception, ObservedGapType* on PlotPointCharacter are different. A scene may involve multiple observed characters, each with their own observation gap. These are not singular. They benefit from being Note instances in an Observed Experience track, carrying individual state, because you may design the AJ-to-Luna gap fully while leaving the AJ-to-Twilight gap in an undesigned state. The Note system handles this correctly. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ What Is Affirmed and Why *POVBelief on PlotPoint.* Affirmed. Name is correct. It captures what the POV character sincerely believes is true about themselves and their situation — the filter through which all narration passes. The distinction from psychological architecture (Track A on Character entity) is that POVBelief is scene-specific and transient: it is what the character believes right now, which may be accurate, deluded, or in transition. Track A is the standing truth about who they are. These are not redundant. The cognitive mode required — inhabiting a consciousness and stating its active working theory — is genuinely distinct from systems engineering or historical documentation. *ReaderState on PlotPoint.* Affirmed as the master reader experience statement for the scene. Name is slightly ambiguous — it could mean "state of the reader" or "stated reader experience" — but it is established enough to keep. Its scope is comprehensive: it covers the full reader experience across all characters in the scene. It is the specification the other Scene Architecture fields serve. *GapType on PlotPoint as an enum.* Affirmed. The classification Ironic, Tragic, Closing, Aligned is correct and complete for the POV self- perception gap. Ironic means the reader perceives more than the character about themselves. Tragic means the character perceives the truth but the reader now understands its weight more fully than the character does. Closing means character and reader converging. Aligned means no productive gap — the character perceives what the reader perceives, appropriate for resolved arcs or pure action sequences. *ArcStatement on PlotPointCharacter.* Affirmed. This is the omniscient arc movement record that enables the chronological scan. Name is clear. It is Stage 1 content — an authorial assertion about what happened to this character's arc at this scene. It is not experience design; it is the record against which experience design is calibrated. *EvidenceDeposited on PlotPointTheme.* Affirmed. Name correctly identifies what the field holds: not a design specification for what the reader should feel but a record of what has been deposited for the reader to reason from. The cognitive mode — author as rhetorical auditor — is distinct from gap design. The failure mode it prevents — theme becoming decoration or sermon — is real and specific. *ReaderHypothesis on PlotPointStoryThread.* Affirmed. Name correctly captures the reader's working theory about the thread rather than what is structurally true. Distinct from ImpactDescription, which is Stage 1 content about what the thread did. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ What Changes *StructuralTruth on PlotPointCharacter needs precise scoping.* In the previous design, StructuralTruth was defined as "the omniscient account of what is happening with this character in this scene." This is correct but needs to be distinguished from character psychology that belongs in Track A on the Character entity itself. The test: does this assertion hold across the whole story, or only in this scene? Chrysalis's narcissism is Track A. The specific way her narcissism manifests in Scene 28.3 — that she chose Eros over Eagleclaw because Eros fears her while Eagleclaw pities her — is scene-specific StructuralTruth. The field is correctly placed on the connection entity and correctly named. What changes is the usage discipline: StructuralTruth should only contain content that is not already captured in Track A. Content that restates standing psychology in a scene- specific context should be a cross-reference to Track A rather than a new assertion. *Commentary on PlotPointCodexEntry splits into two fields.* Commentary was doing two distinct jobs: Stage 1 documentation of how the codex element functions in this scene, and Stage 2 reader experience design for what the reader now understands. These are different assertion types requiring different cognitive modes and carrying different diagnostic meanings when empty. The replacement is: * *StructuralFunction* — Stage 1. What this element is doing in this scene. Why it is present. What structural work it performs. Omniscient, authorial. * *ReaderAccumulation* — Stage 2. What the reader now understands about this element, what they are still missing, what their partial understanding implies. Optional: only required when the element is doing deliberate revelation work. For Mentioned and most ActiveUsage appearances, this field can remain empty without signaling design debt. *ImpactDescription on PlotPointStoryThread should be renamed.* ImpactDescription currently contains mixed Stage 1 and Stage 2 content. The rename clarifies the split: * *ThreadRecord* — Stage 1. What the thread did in this scene from the omniscient perspective. The authorial arc movement assertion for the thread at this moment. * *ReaderHypothesis* — Stage 2. What the reader believes about the thread after this scene. Where their working model of the thread now stands, and where it diverges from structural reality. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ What Is Discarded or Restructured *ObservedBelief, ObservedReaderPerception, ObservedGapType as previously conceived need restructuring.* The previous design placed these as fields on PlotPointCharacter analogous to the POV fields on PlotPoint. With the Note entity system, they become Notes in a dedicated track on PlotPointCharacter — the *Observed Experience track*. This means: Each observed character has a collection of scene architecture notes in their Observed Experience track. For scenes where the character is present but the perception gap is not significant, the track is empty without signaling design debt (because the ObservedGapType would be Aligned). For scenes where the gap is load-bearing, the Notes get populated. The names change slightly for clarity within the Note system: *POVReadOf* replaces ObservedBelief. The name more precisely identifies what this is: the POV character's reading of the observed character in this scene. Not the observed character's self-belief. Not the structural truth. The specific reading produced by the POV character's epistemic limits applied to this character in this moment. *ReaderReadOf* replaces ObservedReaderPerception. Parallel construction to POVReadOf. The reader's reading of this character, which may diverge from POVReadOf. The gap between these two readings is the scene's productive irony for this observed character. *ObservedGapType* retains its name as an enum on these Notes, same values as the POV GapType. *FIDAnchors retains placement on PlotPoint but expands scope.* The previous conception was that FIDAnchors holds physical details for the POV self-perception gap. This is too narrow. FIDAnchors should hold the complete set of non-negotiable concrete details that must be present in the scene — for the POV gap, for significant observed character gaps, and for thematic evidence deposition. A single field on PlotPoint captures all anchors for the scene, with the author noting within the field which gap each anchor serves. This is preferable to per-character anchor fields, which would fragment a unified prose requirement across multiple entities. The name FIDAnchors is retained because it correctly names what these details are for: they are the material anchors that enable Free Indirect Discourse to do the gap work without stating it. The cognitive mode required to write them — asking "what specific physical or behavioral detail must be present so the reader perceives X without being told X" — is distinct from all other fields. The whole plot point would be incomplete if there are no notes in Synopsis track or if the notes aren't in Evaluated state Apr 21 Claude responded: The Answer Is Yes, With Precise Scope Validated unified note architecture across all text fields systematically Validated unified note architecture across all text fields systematically The Answer Is Yes, With Precise Scope The unification is architecturally correct, not overengineering, and now is the right time to do it because you are refactoring anyway. The principle is clean: *all prose-like content becomes Notes with tracks; all structured metadata remains as discrete fields*. The distinction is not about length or importance. It is about whether the content benefits from state tracking, ordering, multiplicity, and diagnostic emptiness signals. Prose-like content does. Structured metadata does not. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ What Becomes Notes Every current text string field that holds authorial assertions — on any entity or connection entity — becomes a Note instance in a named track. The parent entity loses the field. The track gains the note. *On PlotPoint:* Stakes, ReaderState, Synopsis, Outcome, VerbatimText, and all Scene Architecture fields (POVBelief, POVReaderPerception, FIDAnchors) become note tracks. The Outcome note track is singular by design — one note stating what changed, Confirmed when the scene is fully designed. *On PlotPointCharacter:* ArcStatement, StructuralTruth, POVReadOf, ReaderReadOf become note tracks within the connection. *On PlotPointCodexEntry:* StructuralFunction and ReaderAccumulation become note tracks. *On PlotPointStoryThread:* ThreadRecord and ReaderHypothesis become note tracks. *On PlotPointTheme:* EvidenceDeposited becomes a note track. Commentary (the existing field) is replaced by EvidenceDeposited as a note track. *On Character:* Description, Inspiration, Archetype become note tracks alongside the existing Track A, B, C note collections. These were already architecturally note-like — they just lacked the formal structure. *On Chapter:* Summary and Description become note tracks. The Chapter Notes (already free-form) become a formal Development Note track. *On Theme:* Description becomes a note track. The Track A thematic proposition and Track B social commentary fields become explicit note tracks with their types declared. *On StoryThread:* Description becomes a note track. The narrative arc description field becomes a formal note track. *On CodexEntry:* Description becomes a note track. All existing note collections are already structured but gain the formal state and track- type metadata. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ What Stays as Discrete Fields These categories are not content — they are structured metadata, identifiers, or classification data. They cannot benefit from state tracking and should not become notes. *Enums:* GapType, GoalTrajectory, UsageType, Prominence, CharacterRole, CharacterDevImpact, DraftStatus, TensionPhase, CoreDriver, ConflictType, Presentation, ThreadScope, CodexCategory, CodexUsageType, NoteState. These are selections, not assertions. *Identifier fields:* Id, Title, Name on every entity. Title is an identifier string, not prose content. It does not benefit from state tracking. *Ordering and position fields:* OrderInChapter, SortOrder, OrderIndex. These are structural numbers. *Short code fields:* Abbreviation on Theme and CodexEntry when it is a 2-3 character code. ColorHex. These are metadata values, not assertions. *Relationship fields:* All foreign keys, navigation properties, and boolean relationship flags including IsPrimary on PlotPointStoryThread. *Date and time metadata:* LastModified, WorldDate. WorldDate belongs on the Note itself rather than on the entity when it applies to a Historical Record note — the date is a property of the specific assertion, not the entity. *Booleans that are structural state:* IsAnalyzed on GeminiEntry. The NeedsFurtherAnalysis boolean is superseded by NoteState on the Note entity itself. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Singleton Tracks Versus Multi-Note Tracks Some tracks are semantically singular — they should contain exactly one Confirmed note because the content is a unified single assertion per scene or entity. *Singleton tracks:* ReaderState (one master gap statement per scene), Outcome (one change statement per scene), POVBelief (one working theory per POV character per scene), POVReaderPerception (one reader perception statement per scene). The singularity is enforced by the UI — a singleton track displays a single-note editor rather than a list, and warns if a second note is added. At the data layer, the track can technically hold multiple notes, which allows drafting alternatives before confirming one. The Confirmed state enforces the intent: only one note in a singleton track should be Confirmed at a time. A track with two Confirmed notes in a singleton context is a data integrity signal. *Multi-note tracks:* Synopsis (multiple ordered scene beats), FIDAnchors (multiple non-negotiable details), ArcRecord (potentially multiple arc movement assertions if a scene does significant work for a character), ObservedExperience (POVReadOf and ReaderReadOf as separate note instances in the same track). The Synopsis track in particular benefits from multiple notes because a scene blueprint is a sequence. Note 1 is the opening beat. Note 2 is the complication. Note 3 is the turn. Each can be individually Confirmed, Flagged, or Unset. The track's completion state is derived: the Synopsis is Stage 3 Complete when all notes are Confirmed and the track is non-empty. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Completion Signal Architecture Your proposal — a PlotPoint is incomplete if the Synopsis track has no Confirmed notes — extends precisely to every required track. The completion signal is derived from note state, not from a separate status field on the parent entity. The logic per track: *Empty and optional:* No signal. The track is deliberately unused. ReaderAccumulation on a codex reference for a Mentioned usage is correctly empty. *Empty and required:* Diagnostic signal. The track is required but no work has been done. An empty Synopsis track on any scene with a DraftStatus above Idea signals Stage 3 debt. An empty POVBelief track on any scene with a POV character assigned signals Stage 2 debt. *Notes present, none Confirmed:* Work in progress. Content exists but has not been reviewed and stabilized. *At least one Confirmed note:* Track is done. For singleton tracks, exactly one Confirmed note is the target state. Which tracks are required versus optional is a property of the track type, stored as metadata on the track definition rather than per-note. POVBelief is required when a POVCharacter is assigned to the PlotPoint. Synopsis is required when DraftStatus is above Idea. ReaderAccumulation is optional regardless. The dashboard reads these rules and produces a completion profile for each PlotPoint: Stage 1 complete when StructuralTruth notes on all connection entities are Confirmed, Stage 2 complete when all Scene Architecture tracks have Confirmed notes, Stage 3 complete when Synopsis track has Confirmed notes. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Ordering Within Tracks The existing SortOrder field on the Note entity handles this correctly. In a Synopsis track, SortOrder determines the sequence of scene beats. In a FIDAnchors track, SortOrder is advisory — the anchors are a set rather than a sequence, but ordering them by importance or by scene position is useful for scanning. Within singleton tracks, SortOrder is irrelevant but harmless. The UI suppresses the reorder interface for singleton tracks. The SortOrder on Note should be scoped per track and per parent entity. A Note in the Synopsis track of Scene 12.17 has a SortOrder that positions it within that track, not globally across all notes. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Rendering Architecture The UI renders each track type with a specific interface, but the data structure is uniform. Track type metadata drives the rendering decision. *Synopsis track:* Ordered list of notes, each editable in place. Reorder handle visible. DraftStatus indicator on the track header showing completion state. Each note has full state controls. *FIDAnchors track:* Checklist-style rendering. Each anchor note is a discrete item. The checklist framing reinforces the purpose — these are non-negotiable items that must be present in the prose. *Singleton tracks (ReaderState, POVBelief, POVReaderPerception, Outcome):* Single-note editor with prominent display. No list interface. Add button disabled once a Confirmed note exists. *Arc Record track on PlotPointCharacter:* Compact list readable in scanning mode. ArcStatement notes display with enough text to read the arc movement without expanding, because the primary use of this track is the chronological scan, not individual editing. *Observed Experience track on PlotPointCharacter:* Grouped display — POVReadOf and ReaderReadOf notes paired visually, with the GapType enum between them. The pairing reinforces that these two notes exist in relation to each other. *Development Note track:* Lower visual prominence than content tracks. Clearly differentiated as project management content, not story content. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Why This Is Not Overengineering The unification reduces total schema complexity rather than increasing it. The current schema has many differently-named text fields across many models, each requiring a separate migration when the state tracking requirement was identified. The unified Note entity means one schema change — adding NoteState, FlagReason, TrackType, and SortOrder to the existing Note entity — handles the entire system rather than requiring field-by-field additions across a dozen models. The refactoring cost is concentrated in the ViewModel layer, which you are building anyway. The data migration from existing text fields to Note instances is mechanical: each text field value becomes a single Note in its corresponding track, with state set to Unset. No content is lost. The migration is reversible. The rendering complexity is in the UI, but this complexity is currently implicit — different text fields are mentally understood to require different handling, but the application doesn't enforce it. Making the rendering logic explicit per track type converts invisible convention into visible structure, which reduces the cognitive load during data entry rather than increasing it. Please provide me with a concise and complete summary of the conclusions, followed by a plan for making the new model classes Apr 22 Claude responded: Summary of Conclusions Synthesized architectural framework and unified data structure taxonomy Synthesized architectural framework and unified data structure taxonomy Summary of Conclusions The Story Planner Is Two Orthogonal Systems The application is simultaneously a *world bible* (story content) and a *project planner* (work in progress management). These two systems are orthogonal — content type is one axis, stability state is the other. All previous mixing problems stem from conflating them. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Assertion Types Every piece of text belongs to exactly one assertion type. The type determines which track it belongs in. *Story Content* — makes claims about the story's world: * *World Truth* — invariant mechanics, biology, physics, independent of character knowledge * *Historical Record* — objective chronological events with causal chains; carries WorldDate field * *Psychological Architecture* — who a character is and why, independent of story events * *Thematic Proposition* — what the story argues and what evidence must be deposited *Narrative Architecture* — makes decisions about information control: * *Macro Architecture* — when and how world truth enters reader/ character awareness, across chapters or stories * *Scene Architecture* — how a specific scene produces a specific reader experience through a POV lens *Prose* — language the reader encounters: * *Prose Fragment* — captured language candidates, not yet committed * *Committed Prose* — final or near-final text (VerbatimText field) *Project Management* — author instructions to future self: * *Obligation* — work that must be done before a note can be Confirmed * *Revision Directive* — records a structural change and its downstream effects * *Cross-Reference* — dependency or connection not captured in the data model * *Development Note* — reflective record of project evolution and paradigm shifts (replaces "meta-observation") * *Bibliographic Note* — real-world analogues and inspirations; permanent, Track C ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Unified Note Entity *All prose-like text fields across all models become Note instances in named tracks.* Structured metadata (enums, identifiers, ordering fields, booleans, foreign keys) remains as discrete fields. Every Note carries: * *Content* — the assertion * *NoteState* — Unset / Flagged / Confirmed * *FlagReason* — text field, visible only when Flagged, inline on the note * *TrackType* — declares the assertion type, enforced by the containing track * *SortOrder* — scoped per track and parent entity * *WorldDate* — optional, required on Historical Record track notes for timeline view * *SourceMaterialId* — optional, for notes constrained by FiM/EaW/P&K canon *NoteState semantics:* * *Unset* — captured but not reviewed; provisional; not safe as a design axiom * *Flagged* — reviewed and found to require active work; FlagReason must explain why * *Confirmed* — stable within current structural context; safe for downstream design work to depend on * Development Notes use Unset / Reviewed rather than Confirmed, since they are not axioms *Superseded notes* are not a state. When a canonical claim is revised, the old claim moves to the Project Management track as a Revision Directive. The World Truth track contains only current Confirmed claims. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Track Architecture Per Entity *Character entity tracks:* * Track Managed by Macro Architecture notes. Closed by revelation scenes. Neither character nor reader has the correct model until the revelation. *Type B gap* — POV character perception versus reader perception. Managed by Scene Architecture fields. Closed by character development. Reader perceives more than the character through FID. These are structurally different and must not be conflated. Type A is information withholding across the story. Type B is perceptual design within a scene. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Work Blocks Raw Capture → Classification → World Mechanics Construction → Causal History Construction → Character Psychology Construction → Thematic Argumentation Design → Macro Narrative Architecture → Scene Architecture Design → Scene Blueprint → Prose Drafting → Continuity Verification → Structural Revision → Publication and Reader Integration. These are not strictly sequential. Layers 1, 2, and 3 co-develop in spirals. Stage 2 work (Scene Architecture) begins when relevant Layer 1/2/3 content is locally Confirmed. Show more Apr 24 Claude responded: What Kind of Gap This Is Analyzed selective interiority as legitimate third-person limited narrative technique Analyzed selective interiority as legitimate third-person limited narrative technique What Kind of Gap This Is This is not a Type A gap and not a Type B gap as defined. It is a third category that the current taxonomy needs to name precisely. *Type A* — world truth withheld from both character and reader simultaneously. Character and reader share the same wrong model until revelation. *Type B* — POV character perceives less than the reader. The reader, through accumulated structural context, perceives something the character does not about themselves or others. *What you are describing is Type C* — world truth that the POV character possesses but the narrator deliberately withholds from rendering. The character knows. The reader does not. The narrator is choosing not to access the relevant interiority at a specific moment to preserve reader ignorance of something the character could explain if the narrator went there. This is architecturally distinct from both prior types because the asymmetry runs differently. In Type A the character is also ignorant. In Type B the reader knows more. In Type C the character knows more than the reader, and the narrator is the agent of the withholding — not through the character's ignorance but through selective interiority management. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Why Third Person Limited Makes This Possible Your first person instinct is exactly correct and worth making precise. In first person, the narrator *is* the character speaking retrospectively or in real time. The narrative voice and the character's consciousness are identical. When Applejack in first person encounters the ration can and reacts with revulsion, the reader's expectation is that the narrator will explain themselves. A first person narrator who says "I ate the mush unhappily and said nothing more about it" is being conspicuously evasive — the reader perceives the evasion as a craft choice rather than experiencing the mystery naturally. The gap is visible as a gap, which breaks immersion. In third person limited, the narrator observes the character's consciousness *selectively*. The narrator is not the character. The narrator is bounded by the character's experience but is not obligated to render every thought or memory the character has. The narrator can zoom in to deep interiority when the design requires it, and zoom out to behavioral observation when rendering interiority would betray the withheld information. This zoom is the technical mechanism. It has a name: *narrative distance modulation* within third person limited. The narrator moves along a spectrum in a single scene or even a single paragraph: *Deep interiority* (FID, full access): "The metallic taste hit her first. Wrong. Something about it was just wrong, and she couldn't — no. She wasn't doing this now." *Behavioral observation* (zoomed out): "Applejack ate without comment. The can went into her pack when she was done." *Middle register* (emotional tone without source): "She ate it. It wasn't worth discussing." The middle and behavioral registers allow the narrator to convey that an emotional response exists — she dislikes this, there is something freighted about this moment — without accessing the specific memory chain that would explain why. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Design Specification for These Scenes The macro architecture decision is: *the narrator zooms out from AJ's interiority specifically when the ration can appears in any scene where she is the POV character, rendering her response at the behavioral and tonal register rather than the interiority register.* The FIDAnchors for each of these scenes should specify the zoom level explicitly: *Chapter 1 bunker scene:* Behavioral and sensory. The can is described from the outside — its appearance, the sound it makes, the texture of the contents. AJ's reaction is physical: a brief physical tell (the scrunch you mentioned), then suppression, then eating. No memory access. No explanation. The narrator presents the response as complete in itself. *Chapter 2 Comet Shine dialogue:* AJ's "I'd rather not talk about them" is itself a behavioral note — the character performing closure rather than the narrator rendering what is behind the closure. The reader gets the signal that something is there without being given what it is. Crucially, AJ's decision to close the conversation is rendered as deliberate behavior, not as internal reasoning. The narrator does not enter the moment of decision. *Chapter 5 Fluttershy and the animals:* This scene is in Fluttershy's POV rather than AJ's. The design implication here is significant: Fluttershy's reading of AJ's response to the cans will be partial and incorrect, which is a Type B gap. Fluttershy perceives that AJ doesn't like the cans — maybe she reads it as pride about her farm's apples, or distaste for low quality food. The reader, carrying the Chapter 2 seed, senses something more specific is present that Fluttershy is not reading correctly. When the real apples arrive from Ponyville, Fluttershy notices AJ's reaction to them too, and reads it as farm pride or homesickness. This is productive — the reader accumulates two data points (she hates these cans, she loves these particular apples from her farm) that together should produce a hypothesis before Chapter 7 makes it explicit. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Chapter 7 Recontextualization When Apple Bloom's POV in Rarity's chapter visits the factory and the reader discovers Bright Mac and Pear Butter are alive and making standardized rations, the recontextualization works as follows: The reader retroactively replays the ration can scenes and the Comet Shine exchange. The can that AJ scrunched at and ate without comment was made by her parents. The "I'd rather not talk about them" was not generic estrangement or grief — it was the specific pain of parents who chose industry over the farm, whose industrial product she has been eating in wartime without being able to say why it tastes like something she needs to not think about. For the astute reader who hypothesized this before Chapter 7, the revelation is confirmation. For the reader who did not, it is the specific feeling of retroactive inevitability — the clues were there, the design was honest, the withholding was the narrator's selective zoom rather than authorial cheating. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Where This Lives in the System *Macro Architecture track on the Applejack's Parents codex entry:* "The ration can is the planted object for the parents revelation. The narrator zooms out from AJ's interiority when the can appears in Chapters 1 and 5 to preserve Type C withholding. The zoom level is behavioral and tonal, not deep interiority. The Comet Shine dialogue in Chapter 2 is the verbal plant — AJ's closure behavior rather than narrated reasoning. The Chapter 7 revelation from Apple Bloom's POV recontextualizes all prior scenes. This is a retroactive Type B gap for the reader who reads Chapter 7 and replays the prior scenes." *FIDAnchors for each ration can scene:* "Zoom out. Present AJ's response at behavioral register — physical tell, suppression, eating without comment. Do not enter the memory that would explain the response. The emotional tone is present; the source is withheld. The can's description should be sensory and specific enough to be memorable when Chapter 7 recontextualizes it." *POVBelief for AJ in Chapter 1 and Chapter 2 scenes:* "AJ knows exactly what these cans are and who made them. This knowledge is present in her consciousness. The narrator does not render it." *POVGapType for these scenes:* Type C. The POV character possesses knowledge the reader does not. The narrator is the agent of withholding through selective zoom. You said: What other types are possible? What other types are possible? Maybe it's not prudent to specify each one, or is it still worth it? After defining the macro architecture notes for each entity, I imagine going through the connected plot points and putting notes as to what part of the planned macro architecture that particular scene needs to serve for that entity's macro architecture. Then the combined payloads of all entities would be used to inform the plot point specific third person limited design. What are the pros and cons of having the type A-B-C stuff versus general notes? Do I have to plan out the macro architectures first to get a feel? Also it seems like type A/B/C isn't productive naming. What are the fundamentals and can they be specified individually instead of using A/B/C are blanket statements? Apr 24 Claude responded: The Fundamentals Underlying All Gap Types Dissected gap taxonomy fundamentals and weighed enum precision against descriptive prose Dissected gap taxonomy fundamentals and weighed enum precision against descriptive prose The Fundamentals Underlying All Gap Types Rather than A/B/C, the correct frame is to identify the two independent variables that define any gap: *Variable 1 — Who holds the truth?* The world truth exists independently. Each character holds a partial or distorted version. The reader holds whatever the narrative has given them so far. Any gap is a relationship between at least two of these parties. *Variable 2 — What mechanism produces the asymmetry?* Gaps are not just facts about who knows what. They are produced by specific narrative techniques, and naming the technique is what makes the gap designable rather than merely observable. Every gap you will encounter in TLTT is a combination of one knowledge configuration and one mechanism. The combinations that are meaningfully distinct — meaning they require different design decisions and different prose techniques — are the ones worth naming. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Complete Set of Meaningfully Distinct Configurations *Shared Ignorance* Both character and reader lack the truth simultaneously. The world truth exists in layers 1 and 2 but has not been given to either party. The reader and character hold the same incomplete or wrong model together. The mechanism is macro architecture withholding — a deliberate decision not to deposit the information in any scene the reader has encountered. The gap closes through a revelation event that corrects both parties simultaneously. The stagnation beginning in 930 ALB rather than 0 ALB is this configuration. AJ and Twilight believe 1000 years. The reader believes 1000 years alongside them. Chapter 22 corrects both. The design work lives in the Macro Architecture track. The scene-level work is designing the revelation scene's landing. No FID calibration is required to maintain the gap — the gap is maintained by absence, not by technique. *Reader Insight* The reader perceives something about the POV character that the POV character does not perceive about themselves. The reader's accumulated structural context — layer 1 and 2 content, prior scene experiences, established psychology — puts them in a position to see what the character cannot see about their own behavior or psychology. The mechanism is FID calibration combined with structural context accumulation. The character's self-perception is rendered genuinely through FID. The reader's superior position comes from context the character lacks about themselves, not from information the narrator is withholding. AJ at the machine gun believing she has found her proper place is this configuration. The narrator is not withholding anything. The reader simply has structural context that AJ does not have about her own behavior. The design work lives in POVBelief and POVReaderPerception on the PlotPoint, and in FIDAnchors specifying what physical details anchor the reader's inference without stating it. *Character Possession* The POV character holds information the reader does not have. The narrator, through selective zoom, chooses not to render the interiority that would deliver this information to the reader. The character's knowledge is present in their consciousness but the narrator does not go there. The mechanism is narrative distance modulation — zooming from deep FID interiority to behavioral or tonal register at precisely the moments when rendering interiority would betray the withheld content. The ration can scenes are this configuration. AJ knows. The narrator does not enter the memory that would explain why she knows. The design work lives in the Macro Architecture track (the governing decision) and in FIDAnchors specifying the zoom level — behavioral register here, tonal register here, do not enter interiority here. *Reader Misdirection* The reader is actively led toward a wrong model that will later be corrected. This is distinct from Shared Ignorance because the reader's wrong model is not simply incomplete — it is positively constructed by the narrative. The reader forms a hypothesis and the narrative is designed to make that hypothesis feel like the correct explanation. The mechanism is controlled evidence deposition. The reader is given real evidence that points toward a false conclusion, then given corrective evidence later that recontextualizes the earlier evidence as having pointed somewhere else all along. The correction should feel retroactively inevitable — the reader should be able to trace back through the planted evidence and see that the true answer was consistent with it. The design work requires explicit identification in the Macro Architecture track of what false model is being constructed and what evidence plants it, so that the corrective revelation can be designed to make all prior evidence rereadable. *Observed Asymmetry* The POV character observes another character and forms a reading of them. The reader, holding structural context the POV character lacks, perceives something about the observed character that the POV character misses. This is the observed version of Reader Insight — the gap is not about the POV character's self-perception but about their perception of someone else. The mechanism is POV character epistemic limits applied to observed behavior. Mali reading Luna's resistance as guilt when it is actually fear of recurrence is this configuration. Mali's reading is sincere and partially correct. The reader, having structural context about Luna that Mali doesn't have, perceives the deeper truth. The design work lives in POVReadOf and ReaderReadOf on PlotPointCharacter, specifying both readings explicitly so the FID can render Mali's sincere but incomplete perception while the physical details give the reader purchase on the fuller truth. *Multi-POV Synthesis* The reader, having occupied multiple POV positions across multiple scenes, synthesizes a composite understanding that no single character possesses. Each POV character has access to a partial truth. The reader holds all the partial truths simultaneously and can see their relationship. No character can see what the reader sees because no character has been inside multiple other characters' consciousness. The mechanism is the multi-POV structure itself. The Mali and Twilight back-to-back POV design for the Dreamscape Aid Network scenes is this configuration — Mali reads the operation through relationship, Twilight reads it through framework, and the reader synthesizes a composite understanding of what the operation is and what it costs that neither character has access to individually. The design work lives in the Chapter Reader Arc track — this is a chapter-level design decision rather than a scene-level one. The composite the reader is meant to synthesize must be specified at the chapter level, then each scene's POV is designed to deposit its partial contribution. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Whether to Formalize These as an Enum The GapType enum on PlotPoint and ObservedGapType on PlotPointCharacter handles the scene-level cases. These enums should be kept as they are — they classify the relationship between character and reader at the moment of the scene. The macro-level configurations — Shared Ignorance, Character Possession, Reader Misdirection, Multi-POV Synthesis — should *not* become an enum. The reasons are precise. These configurations are not mutually exclusive within a single entity's arc. The ration can story simultaneously involves Character Possession (AJ knows, narrator withholds) and Reader Misdirection (the reader may construct a wrong model about why AJ is estranged from her parents before Chapter 7 corrects them). Adding an enum forces a single classification onto content that is inherently composite. They also don't attach cleanly to a single entity or scene. Shared Ignorance is a property of the reader-story relationship across many scenes. Multi-POV Synthesis is a property of a chapter structure. Character Possession requires specifying which scenes are governed by the zoom decision and where the revelation falls. These are descriptions of information architecture patterns, not classifications of individual data points. The correct representation is *vocabulary used in Macro Architecture notes* rather than enum values. A Macro Architecture note on the Applejack's Parents codex entry that says "Character Possession maintained through Chapters 1-6 via selective zoom; Reader Misdirection may occur if reader hypothesizes parental death before Chapter 7 recontextualization; Shared Ignorance is not in play because AJ knows throughout" is more precise and more actionable than any enum classification of that note. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Whether to Design Macro Architecture First Yes. The dependency runs in one direction: scene-level gap design presupposes macro architecture decisions for the same entity. You cannot correctly specify the zoom level in AJ's ration can FIDAnchors without having first decided in the Macro Architecture track that Character Possession is the governing strategy for the parents arc through Chapter 6. You cannot correctly design the POVReadOf for Mali's perception of Luna without having first decided in Luna's Macro Architecture track that her true psychology is being maintained in Reader Insight rather than revealed directly until Scene 22.12. The Macro Architecture note specifies the governing strategy. The scene- level design fields execute that strategy at specific moments. If the governing strategy is not documented first, each scene-level decision is made without reference to the overall information architecture, and the cumulative effect will be inconsistent — some scenes will accidentally advance information that should be withheld, others will over-withhold when partial revelation was intended. The practical workflow is: For each significant entity — every character with a revelatory arc, every codex element with a controlled disclosure plan, every world truth being withheld until a specific point — write the Macro Architecture note first. This note should specify which configuration is in play, what information is being withheld from whom, across which chapter range, and what the intended revelation point is. It should also specify what evidence is being planted to enable reader hypothesis formation and retroactive inevitability. Then, when designing individual scenes, the scene-level design fields execute the strategy the Macro Architecture note has already decided. The FIDAnchors for a Character Possession scene says "zoom out here" because the Macro Architecture note already said "maintain Character Possession through Chapter 6." The POVBelief for a Reader Insight scene is calibrated against the Macro Architecture note that specified which structural context the reader should be carrying by this point. The Macro Architecture notes are therefore the bridge between layers 1, 2, and 3 — which are now locally Confirmed through your ongoing work — and the scene-level design phase that has not yet begun. Writing the Macro Architecture notes for significant entities is the correct first task of Stage 2 work, before any individual scene's POVBelief or FIDAnchors is written. You said: How does Gérard Genette's formal term "focalization" factor into the discussions we've had? How does Gérard Genette's formal term "focalization" factor into the discussions we've had? There is zero focalization, internal focalization and external focalization It seems like I would want to avoid zero focalization because that would be revealing world truths and history instead of the characters experiencing it. Would the example of Applejack in the trench stating this is where she is contributing most, but the reader sensing that she is hiding from duty, an example of utilizing internal focalization to create a gap between the reader and the POV character? Would the example of Applejack disliking the canned mush but the narrator not diving into why be an example of external focalization? So it is variable focalization not only by changing characters (Applejack in Tall Tale, Rarity in Manehattan) but also variable regarding when to use internal and when to use external? As long as there is a story design reason to do so and it is not arbitrary. The design reasons stem from the macro architecture of the entities. Show more Apr 24 Claude responded: Genette's Focalization and Its Relationship to the Framework Genette's Focalization and Its Relationship to the Framework Genette's framework maps precisely onto what we have been building, and naming the terms correctly sharpens the design vocabulary. *Zero focalization* is the omniscient narrator who knows more than any character. The narrator has access to world truth, history, and all psychological architectures simultaneously. This is what your Stage 1 content is written in — the assertional mode of world mechanics, historical record, and psychological architecture notes. Zero focalization in prose is what happens when Stage 1 content leaks into the narration without being filtered through a character's epistemic position. Your existing Synopses are written in zero focalization. The failure mode this conversation has been diagnosing — structural truths appearing directly in the narration as if no POV character filters them — is precisely zero focalization masquerading as third person limited. You are correct that TLTT should avoid zero focalization in prose. The Stage 1 content exists as the author's reference material. It never appears directly in the narration. It is the ground truth against which focalized narration is calibrated. *Internal focalization* is what Genette calls the mode where the narrator gives no more information than a specific character knows — and specifically, where the narrator renders the character's perception of events including their inner life. The narrator is bounded by and co- extensive with the character's consciousness. Your POVBelief, POVReaderPerception, and FIDAnchors fields are all design tools for managing internal focalization. The FID technique operates entirely within internal focalization — it renders the character's thoughts and perceptions in the third person without attribution tags. Your AJ in the trench example is correct. The narrator renders AJ's genuine contentment through internal focalization — the reader is inside her consciousness, experiencing her self-perception as she experiences it. The gap between her self-perception and the reader's inference is not produced by stepping outside her focalization. It is produced by the reader's structural context operating on the internally focalized narration. The focalization remains internal. The irony is produced by what the reader brings to that internal focalization from outside the scene. *External focalization* is the mode where the narrator gives less information than the character knows — describing behavior, physical response, and observable action without accessing interiority. The camera observes from outside. The character's inner life is present but not rendered. Your ration can example is precisely correct. The narrator describes AJ's physical response — the scrunch, the eating without comment — while remaining outside her interiority. The narrator knows AJ's inner life is present and is choosing not to render it. This is external focalization deployed strategically to maintain Character Possession. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Variable Focalization as the Governing Design Principle Yes. Variable focalization is the master technique, and your articulation of it is precise: variation occurs both between characters (AJ in Tall Tale, Rarity in Manehattan) and within a single character's scenes (internal when the design requires interiority, external when the design requires withholding). Genette calls this *variable focalization* when it shifts between characters and *paralepsis* when the narrator temporarily exceeds the character's knowledge. The inverse — the narrator giving less information than the character possesses — is what Genette calls *paralipsis*. Your ration can scenes are paralipsis: the narrator deliberately gives less than the focalizing character knows. The critical design principle you have identified is that these variations must be *motivated by macro architecture decisions, not arbitrary*. Unmotivated external focalization reads as evasion — the narrator conspicuously not going somewhere, which signals to the reader that the narrator is hiding something in a craft-visible way that breaks immersion. Motivated external focalization reads as behavioral observation — the narrator presenting a character whose inner life is present but contained, which the reader experiences as a property of the character rather than a property of the narration. The difference between evasion and motivation comes from what surrounds the external focalization moment. If the scene around AJ's ration can response has been internally focalized — we have been inside her thoughts about other things, the narration has rendered her consciousness freely — then the zoom-out at the can moment is felt as the character's own suppression rather than the narrator's evasion. AJ is not thinking about this. That is itself information, rendered through the contrast between internal access elsewhere and external register here. The reader reads the zoom-out as AJ's choice to not think about it, not as the narrator hiding something. This is why the FIDAnchors for Character Possession scenes must specify not just "zoom out here" but also "ensure internal focalization in the surrounding material so the zoom-out reads as the character's suppression rather than narrator evasion." ------------------------------------------------------------------------ How This Maps to the Field Architecture Genette's terminology gives precise names to what the design fields are governing: *POVBelief* specifies what the internally focalized narration should render as the character's consciousness. This is the content of internal focalization for this scene. *FIDAnchors* specifies the concrete objects and behaviors through which internal focalization is expressed without interiority tags. FID is the prose technique of internal focalization. The anchors are the specific material elements the technique must include. *POVGapType* — the Reader Insight configuration — corresponds to internal focalization producing a gap through structural irony. The focalization is internal, the irony is produced by context. *Character Possession in Macro Architecture* — corresponds to paralipsis: internal focalization for the scene generally, external focalization at specific moments where interiority would betray the withheld information. *ObservedExperience track* — governs focalization of another character through the POV character's perception. The POV character is the focalizer; the observed character is the focalized object. POVReadOf is what the focalizer's internal access produces about the observed character. ReaderReadOf is what the reader synthesizes from the same internally focalized material, given structural context the focalizer lacks. *Zero focalization* is the mode of Stage 1 notes and Macro Architecture notes. The author writes in zero focalization. The narrator never does. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Practical Implication for Scene Design The scene design question is now expressible with precise vocabulary: for each moment in a scene, what focalization level is the narrator operating at, and is that level motivated by the macro architecture decision for the relevant entity? A scene design note in FIDAnchors can now say: "Internal focalization throughout except at the ration can moment. Zoom to external at the can. The zoom reads as AJ's suppression because the surrounding internal focalization establishes the narrator's access to her consciousness elsewhere in the scene." A Macro Architecture note on Applejack's Parents can now say: "Paralipsis governs all ration can scenes in Chapters 1-6. The narrator maintains internal focalization access to AJ in these scenes but deploys external focalization at the specific moment of the can, producing the Character Possession gap through motivated zoom-out." The focalization level is not a binary across an entire scene or chapter. It is a variable that the narrator modulates continuously, and each modulation should correspond to a design decision traceable to a macro architecture motivation. The story planner is the system that holds those motivations explicitly so that prose drafting can modulate focalization deliberately rather than accidentally. You said: It seems like "third person limited" is often conflated to strict internal focalization, with the opposite pole of "third person omniscient" taken to mean zero… It seems like "third person limited" is often conflated to strict internal focalization, with the opposite pole of "third person omniscient" taken to mean zero focalization. Given that I was already planning on both the trench scene and the can scene in my instinctive, messy raw capture, I already had in mind a plan to utilize both internal focalization and external via paralipsis, along with variable focalization via different characters to produce structural and dramatic irony.
