Show more Apr 25 Claude responded: Countries Are Not Codex Entries Countries Are Not Codex Entries Your framework has already produced the correct answer. A country is not a single entity at any of the four category types — it is a *label applied to a geographic region that encompasses multiple Institutions, Organizations, and World Principles simultaneously*. Treating a country as a single codex entry is the same category error as having a single note that mixes World Truth, Historical Record, and Project Management content. The conflation is the problem. The EaW game map uses country labels as a convenient shorthand for political control of territory. Your story planner should not inherit this conflation. "Equestria" as a codex entry has no Ontology, no Governing Logic, no Binding Logic, and no Mechanics — because Equestria is not a World Principle, Institution, Technology, or Organization. It is a geographic and political label that points at a collection of those things. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ What Countries Decompose Into *Equestria* decomposes as: Institutions: Stagnation of Harmony (governing policy), Cutie Mark System (as Celestia has institutionalized it), Celestia's Solar Bureaucracy (the administrative framework for running the state) Organizations: EEEE, Star Energy Corporation, The Equestrian Army (what remains of it), The rural majority as a demographic grouping, The Manehattan industrial compradors World Principles: Earth pony magic, Pegasi weather manipulation biology, Unicorn magic scaling, Alicorn special talent mechanics — these are not Equestrian inventions, they are World Principles that happen to operate prominently within Equestria's territory Characters: Celestia, Luna, Blueblood as the political actors whose psychology constitutes the state's decision-making The geographic fact — that these entities are all located within a specific territory — is a Historical Record note on each relevant entity: "operates within Equestrian territory" or "founded in Manehattan." Geography is a property of entities, not an entity in itself. *Changeling Lands* decomposes as: Institutions: MEFO Bills, Great Leap Forward (Chrysalis's industrialization policy), Love Tax System, VOPS (secret police framework), Statthalter System, Bauleiter System Organizations: Changeling Heer, Statthalters as an organization distinct from the Statthalter System institution, Jaegers, Drone worker class World Principles: Changeling biology (love as sustenance, metamorphosis, VOPS emotional sensing), Hive genetic structure Characters: Chrysalis, Trimmel, Pagala, Synovial as the decision-making actors The decomposition reveals that "Changeling Lands" is really Chrysalis's personal project — a set of Institutions she constructed and Organizations she commands, all grounded in World Principles she is exploiting. The geographic territory is incidental. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ What To Do With Existing Country Entries Your existing data likely has entries titled "Equestria," "Griffonian Republic," "Stalliongrad," etc. These should not be deleted — they should be converted to *Geographic Reference* entries whose only content is: A brief descriptor of what territory the label covers, used as a navigation aid. A list of the Institutions, Organizations, and Characters that constitute what the label points at, as cross-references via the obligation mechanism until those entities are formally connected through plot points. Notes on how the geographic label is used and misused within the story — which is where the narrative irony you identified lives. The Geographic Reference is not a fifth codex category. It is a navigational container. Its primary track is not Ontology or Governing Logic or Mechanics or Binding Logic — it is an index that points outward to the entities that constitute the actual content. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Narrative Irony as a Designed Gap The conflation of country label with the entities it contains is not just a data modeling problem. It is one of your most productive Type B gaps. AJ and Twilight, for the first two-thirds of the story, perceive "The Aquileians" as a unified entity with unified motivations. Henri, Fleur, Reni, and Minette are all "Aquileian" in AJ's internally focalized perception. The FID renders their nationality as a coherent identity. The reader, accumulating structural context from the world-building content deposited through these characters' appearances, begins to sense that Henri's Auftragstaktik discipline and Reni's chasseur code and Fleur's Coltbertian economics and Minette's cartel survival are not expressions of a unified "Aquileian" identity but products of distinct Institutions and Organizations within a territory whose label papers over internal contradictions. The reader perceives the decomposition. AJ perceives the label. When AJ goes to Aquileia and discovers the Aquileian Cartel, the urban thugs, the FJA rural culture, the coastal industrial cities, and the mountain terroir economy — the Type B gap closes violently. She has been fighting alongside "Aquileia" and discovers it contains everything she has been fighting against. The resolution is not that Aquileia is bad — it is that the label was always insufficient and the entities within it are what actually matter. This gap should be designed explicitly in AJ's Track A epistemic limits: "AJ generalizes from representatives to wholes. She perceives Henri as 'Aquileia' rather than as 'the FJA military tradition within Aquileia.' This generalizing epistemic limit produces the productive misread of 'The Aquileians' as a unified entity until Chapter X confronts her with the contradiction." The Macro Architecture track on each significant Aquileian character entry should note: "AJ reads this character as representative of Aquileia as a whole. This reading is internally consistent with AJ's epistemic limits and is productive — it will be retroactively recontextualized when AJ encounters Aquileia's internal contradictions in Chapter X." The Geographic Reference entry for Aquileia's Project Management track should contain an obligation: "The AJ-reads-Aquileia-as-unified gap must be tracked across all Aquileian character appearances in Chapters 2-25 to ensure the misconception is consistently maintained in AJ POV scenes and consistently perceptible as incomplete by the reader through structural context." ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Cross-Cutting That Countries Enable Countries in EaW and HoI4 are the primary cross-cutting mechanism — every event, character, and mechanic is tagged to a country, and the country label is what allows you to ask "what is happening in Equestria right now." Your story planner needs an equivalent cross-cutting mechanism that is more precise than country labels. The correct substitute is *tagging entities with their institutional context* rather than their geographic nationality. A character is not "Equestrian" — they are "operates within Star Energy, aligned with EEEE's ideology, product of Manehattan's industrial displacement." The tags are their institutional affiliations and the Organizations they belong to. The chronological history view already provides geographic cross-cutting through the timeline. Events tagged with WorldDate that involve entities geographically located in the same territory will naturally cluster in the timeline view. This is the correct mechanism — geography as a temporal filtering axis rather than a categorical classification axis. What this means practically is that the existing Nation entries in your CodexCategory enum should be retired as a category. Nations are Geographic References — navigational containers that point at Institutions, Organizations, Characters, and World Principles, not entries with their own ontological content. The existing data in Nation entries should be migrated: Governing Logic content moves to Institution entries, Binding Logic content moves to Organization entries, World Principle content moves to World Principle entries, and the Nation entry itself becomes a lean Geographic Reference with an index and the irony- tracking notes described above. You said: What should be the note tracks within each of the 4 codex categories' connections to plot points? What should be the note tracks within each of the 4 codex categories' connections to plot points? Apr 25 Claude responded: The Design Principle The Design Principle Each connection entity's note tracks answer two questions in sequence: what is objectively happening with this entity in this scene (Stage 1, zero focalization), and how does this appearance serve the reader's experience (Stage 2, scene architecture). The Stage 1 tracks differ by category because what constitutes the objective record of a World Principle's appearance is fundamentally different from what constitutes the objective record of an Organization's appearance. The Stage 2 tracks are more consistent because reader experience design follows the same logic regardless of entity type. The existing connection entity *PlotPointCodexEntry* currently serves all codex categories with a single Commentary field and UsageType enum. The redesign gives each category distinct Stage 1 tracks while sharing Stage 2 tracks, because the objective record varies by category but the reader experience architecture does not. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ PlotPointCodexEntry: World Principle *UsageType enum* — retained, but values should be recalibrated for World Principles specifically. A World Principle appearance is rarely just Mentioned — it is more precisely: Implied (the principle is operating but no character perceives it), Observed (a character observes a consequence without naming the principle), Named (a character articulates the principle explicitly), Subverted (the principle appears to be violated, setting up later correction), or Demonstrated (the principle's operation is shown directly producing a consequence the reader can verify). *Stage 1 tracks:* *Operational Instance* — zero focalization record of how the World Principle is actually operating in this scene. Not what characters perceive about it. What it is actually doing. "The charitostatic effect is producing a measurable output differential between the two armies in this scene. The Equestrian volunteers' shared solidarity is generating a combined magical output that neither army individually can match. This is the physical mechanism underlying the tactical outcome." This track is the author's confirmation that the World Principle is genuinely present and operative in this scene, not merely referenced. An empty Operational Instance with a non-empty Stage 2 track is a design integrity signal — you are designing reader experience around a World Principle that is not actually operating in the scene. *Stage 2 tracks:* *ReaderAccumulation* — what the reader now understands about this World Principle after this scene, what they are still missing, and what their partial understanding implies. Retained from the earlier framework. Optional — only required when the appearance is doing deliberate revelation work toward a major disclosure. A scene where earth pony magic is incidentally present but not being revealed needs no ReaderAccumulation note. A scene where a character's observation plants a seed for the charitostatic revelation needs one. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ PlotPointCodexEntry: Constructed Idea *UsageType enum* — different values are needed. A Constructed Idea's appearances are: Referenced (a character mentions it without engaging), Invoked (a character applies its principles to a situation), Challenged (a character tests or disputes its claims), Demonstrated (events show the Constructed Idea producing its predicted consequences), Subverted (events show the Constructed Idea failing or being misapplied), Revealed (the Constructed Idea's full scope is disclosed for the first time or substantially advanced). *Stage 1 tracks:* *Invocation Record* — zero focalization record of how the Constructed Idea is being used in this scene and by whom. "Fleur invokes Coltbert's Asset Specificity framework to explain why Comet Shine's Skyfall training makes him dangerous as a negotiating partner. She is applying the framework correctly. The irony is that she is herself an example of Asset Specificity operating outside Coltbert's anticipated parameters — a communist-adjacent Aquileian invoking an ego-capitalist framework." This differs from Operational Instance on World Principle because a Constructed Idea does not automatically operate — it must be invoked by an agent. The record documents who is invoking it, whether they are applying it correctly, and what the objective consequence of the invocation is in the scene's causal chain. *Misapplication Note* — optional, Stage 1. When a character invokes the Constructed Idea incorrectly, this documents the nature and significance of the error in zero focalization. "Applejack applies Coltbert's Sovereignty of Consent framework to the drug deal decision, invoking it to justify that Skyfall workers chose the rat race. This is a misapplication — Coltbert's framework requires genuine alternatives to exist for consent to be meaningful. Skyfall workers under Chrysalis's economic capture do not have genuine alternatives. AJ is using the framework's vocabulary to rationalize a decision whose emotional driver is the personal grievance about her parents." This track is empty in most scenes. When populated, it is a direct input to the GapType design in Stage 2. *Stage 2 tracks:* *ReaderAccumulation* — same as World Principle. The reader's current model of the Constructed Idea and what remains withheld. For Coltbert's paper specifically, the ReaderAccumulation tracks across all its appearances map the controlled disclosure arc from Fleur's Chapter 2 invocation through Twilight reading it in Chapter 26 — each entry documenting how much of the paper's content the reader has been given and what the partial understanding implies about prior events. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ PlotPointCodexEntry: Technology *UsageType enum* — the existing enum is already well-calibrated for Technology: Mentioned, ActiveUsage, Definition, Subversion, Failure. These values correctly describe the range of ways a Technology can appear. Retain with one addition: *FirstReveal* for the scene where the technology is shown to the reader for the first time, which has specific design obligations around establishing the physical reality of the object. *Stage 1 tracks:* *Deployment Record* — zero focalization record of how the technology is physically operating in this scene. What it is doing, what its inputs are, what its outputs are, whether it is functioning correctly or under stress. "The Luna Nova Rifle is operating at 73% efficiency due to the operators' emotional state — the charitostatic drain on the love canister is higher than designed because the operators are using the rifle from a state of fear rather than solidarity. This is not visible to the characters but is the physical explanation for the tactical outcome of the scene." This track is what prevents Technology from appearing in scenes as a magic prop — it forces the author to document how the technology is actually functioning according to its established Mechanics, creating a consistency check between the Mechanics track on the Technology entry and the scene-specific deployment. *Physical Presence Note* — optional, Stage 1. Required only on FirstReveal appearances. Documents the physical reality of the technology as it exists in this scene — its appearance, weight, sound, smell, the specific physical details that the prose must establish so the reader has a concrete model. "The Luna Nova Rifle is the weight of a standard infantry rifle. The crystal housing along the barrel glows faintly when charged. The draining function produces a specific sound — a low resonance rather than a crack. These physical details must be established in this scene because they will be referenced when Celestia reacts to seeing it in Chapter 22." *Stage 2 tracks:* *ReaderAccumulation* — the reader's current model of what this technology is and can do, and what remains undisclosed. Particularly important for technologies whose full capabilities are revealed gradually — the Love Harvester, the Luna Nova Rifle, the Universal Translator all have disclosure arcs where early appearances give the reader partial understanding that is retroactively expanded. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ PlotPointCodexEntry: Organization *UsageType enum* — Organizations appear differently from the other categories because they are constituted by membership and collective action. Useful values: Represented (one or more members appear as representatives of the organization), Acting Collectively (the organization takes a collective action in this scene), Referenced (discussed without members present), Structurally Invoked (the organization's Binding Logic is what explains a character's behavior in this scene, even if the organization is not explicitly mentioned), Fractured (internal tension within the organization is revealed). *Stage 1 tracks:* *Collective Record* — zero focalization record of what the organization is doing in this scene and how its Binding Logic is operating. "EEEE is represented here by Flowing Current. His behavior in this negotiation is entirely consistent with the organization's Binding Logic — he is applying the solidarity framework to identify class allies among the Tzinacatl drug lords. The scene's objective significance for EEEE is that this negotiation extends the organization's moral framework beyond its established membership boundaries for the first time." This is distinct from a character note about Flowing Current — it documents what the organization as a collective entity is doing through him, not what he is doing as an individual. *Binding Logic Instance* — optional, Stage 1. Required when the scene reveals something new about what holds the organization together or when internal tension threatens the binding. "Rarity's insistence on custom work over assembly line production in this scene creates the first visible fracture in Star Energy's operational consensus. The organization's Binding Logic — harmonic capitalism as a governing principle — is being tested by the tension between Rarity's asset- specific approach and the war's material demands for standardized output. This fracture is not yet at the level of organizational crisis but is the first structural crack." *Stage 2 tracks:* *ReaderAccumulation* — the reader's current model of what this organization is, what holds it together, and what its internal tensions are. For organizations with significant disclosure arcs — EEEE's true ideological foundation, Star Energy's actual ownership structure, the Changeling Heer's internal meritocracy debate — this track maps the controlled disclosure across appearances. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Shared Stage 2 Track: ReaderAccumulation All four categories share ReaderAccumulation as the Stage 2 track because the reader experience design question is the same regardless of entity type: what does the reader now understand about this entity, what are they still missing, and what does their partial understanding imply? The content differs by category — the reader accumulates understanding of how a World Principle operates, or what a Constructed Idea argues, or what a Technology can do, or what holds an Organization together — but the design question and the cognitive mode required are identical. A single shared track name correctly signals this commonality while the category determines what content the field holds. ReaderAccumulation is optional across all four categories. The emptiness signal is: this appearance is not doing deliberate revelation work. The population signal is: this appearance is a designed step in the controlled disclosure arc for this entity. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Connection Entity Structure Summarized Track World Principle Constructed Idea Technology Organization UsageType Implied/Observed/Named/Subverted/Demonstrated Referenced/ Invoked/Challenged/Demonstrated/Subverted/Revealed Mentioned/ ActiveUsage/Definition/Subversion/Failure/FirstReveal Represented/Acting Collectively/Referenced/Structurally Invoked/Fractured Stage 1 primary Operational Instance Invocation Record Deployment Record Collective Record Stage 1 optional — Misapplication Note Physical Presence Note (FirstReveal only) Binding Logic Instance Stage 2 shared ReaderAccumulation ReaderAccumulation ReaderAccumulation ReaderAccumulation The Stage 1 primary tracks have different names because they describe structurally different things: a World Principle operates, a Constructed Idea is invoked, a Technology is deployed, an Organization acts collectively. The names encode the correct cognitive mode — when writing an Operational Instance, you are thinking as a physicist. When writing an Invocation Record, you are thinking as a rhetorician tracking how an argument is being used. When writing a Deployment Record, you are thinking as an engineer verifying mechanical consistency. When writing a Collective Record, you are thinking as a sociologist documenting institutional behavior. You said: I feel like I'm getting stuck on getting everything right instead of building the UI and interacting with real data. I feel like I'm getting stuck on getting everything right instead of building the UI and interacting with real data. The point of making all text data a unified note structure is to allow changing tracks and visualizing them. I'm going to go ahead and built the UI so I can start categorizing real raw data. Please suggest some practical methodogy principles for knowing when a new category is needed. I don't want to end up still with mixed data Apr 25 Claude responded: The Core Principle Recognized user's readiness to build and prioritized practical categorization guidance Recognized user's readiness to build and prioritized practical categorization guidance The Core Principle A new category is needed when existing categories would require you to mentally override their name every time you use them. If you find yourself writing "World Principle" at the top of a note and then writing content that feels like it belongs somewhere else, that friction is the signal. If the existing category name feels right and the content flows naturally, the category is correct. Do not create a new category because you can imagine content that might not fit perfectly. Create one only when you have actual content in hand that produces genuine friction against all existing categories. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Five Practical Signals *Signal 1 — The Name Override.* You open a note, assign it to a track, and immediately feel you need to explain to yourself why it belongs there despite the name feeling wrong. This is the clearest signal. If you are rationalizing the placement rather than recognizing it, the category structure needs revision. *Signal 2 — The Scan Breaks.* You open a track to scan it and find yourself mentally skipping certain notes because they are in a different cognitive mode than the others. A World Principle track that contains some discovered regularities and some constructed policies breaks the scan — your eye has to shift cognitive mode mid-read. When the scan breaks, a category boundary has been violated. *Signal 3 — The Same Question Gets Different Answers.* Each track should answer exactly one question. If you find yourself asking "what does this note answer?" and the answer is "it depends on how you read it," the note contains two assertion types and needs to be split, not recategorized. *Signal 4 — Three Instances Make a Pattern.* One note that does not fit cleanly is probably a malformed note, not evidence of a missing category. Two notes that do not fit might be coincidence. Three notes with the same misfit quality and the same assertion type that none of your existing categories captures — that is evidence of a genuine category gap. *Signal 5 — The Tooltip Would Mislead.* Each track has a tooltip describing what belongs there. If you have a note where following the tooltip would produce worse content than ignoring it, the category is either wrong for this note or the category itself needs refinement. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Practical Workflow When a note feels wrong in its current track, do not immediately create a new category. Run it through this sequence: First, ask whether the note is malformed — whether it contains two assertion types that should be split. Most misfit notes are malformed rather than genuinely uncategorizable. Split it and both halves usually find their home. Second, ask whether the note belongs in a Project Management track rather than a content track. A surprising amount of apparent categorical confusion dissolves when you recognize that a note is really the author talking to themselves rather than making a claim about the story. Third, ask whether the note belongs in Track C (Bibliographic) — whether it is real-world context informing the story rather than an assertion about the story itself. Real-world parallels, historical inspirations, and authorial meta-observations frequently masquerade as world content. Only if the note survives all three checks — it is not malformed, not project management, not bibliographic — and still does not fit any existing category, flag it with a specific tag meaning "category candidate" and continue working. Accumulate these flags. Review them when you have three or more with the same character. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ What Good Enough Looks Like The goal is not a perfect taxonomy before building. The goal is a taxonomy that is stable enough that the UI you build today will not require fundamental restructuring in three months. The current four content categories plus the shared Stage 1/Stage 2 track structure meets this bar. The remaining open questions — exact UsageType enum values per category, whether Geographic Reference deserves a formal entry type, precise edge cases between World Principle and Constructed Idea for things like game- theoretic regularities — are refinements that can be resolved through contact with real data without requiring structural changes. The data model accommodates them because notes can be retracked and enums can gain values without migration. Build with the four categories and the note structure as designed. When real data produces a genuine Signal 3 or Signal 4, stop and resolve it before the pattern spreads to many notes. One bad categorization in ten notes is noise. One bad categorization in a hundred notes is debt. The imperfect taxonomy actively used produces better outcomes than the perfect taxonomy perpetually refined. You said: So for each note track in an entity should it have a tooltip that is verbose but then also display/helper text that is visible which just states the simple que… So for each note track in an entity should it have a tooltip that is verbose but then also display/helper text that is visible which just states the simple question that the notes of that note type should answer? And the track is exclusive, not inclusive. Notes that don't answer that specific question stay in Unset Apr 25 Claude responded: Yes. Yes. This is the correct UI architecture and the reasoning behind each component is worth stating precisely so the implementation reflects the intent. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Two Layers Serve Different Cognitive Moments *The tooltip* is consulted during onboarding and when genuinely uncertain. It is verbose because it needs to establish the cognitive mode, explain what belongs and what does not, and give at least one concrete example. You read it deliberately. It is reference material. *The display question* is active during data entry. It is visible at all times without interaction. It should be short enough to hold in working memory while writing — ideally under fifteen words. It is not a summary of the tooltip. It is the single question the note must answer to belong in this track. You internalize it and it becomes automatic. The relationship between them: the tooltip explains why the question is the right one. The display question is the operational test you apply to every note before assigning it. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Exclusive Track Principle The track is exclusive rather than inclusive. This is the correct framing and it changes how you evaluate borderline notes. An inclusive track asks: could this note plausibly belong here? This produces drift — notes accumulate in the nearest plausible container rather than the correct one. An exclusive track asks: does this note answer this specific question and no other? Notes that answer a different question or answer multiple questions simultaneously do not belong here regardless of subject matter proximity. They belong either in a different track or in Unset pending clarification. Unset is not a failure state. It is the correct holding state for notes whose assertion type has not been determined. A note about Coltbert's paper that mixes a governing logic claim with a historical record claim and a bibliographic observation belongs in Unset until it is split into three notes that each answer one question precisely. Forcing it into the nearest track to clear the Unset queue produces the mixing problem you are trying to solve. The practical discipline is: when assigning a note to a track, ask the display question out loud about the note. If the answer is yes, assign it. If the answer is anything other than a clear yes, it goes to Unset. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Example Display Questions and Tooltips *Ontology track on World Principle entries* Display question: /What is invariantly true about how this operates, regardless of who knows it?/ Tooltip: /State the foundational mechanics of this World Principle as discovered fact — how it operates, what it produces, what constrains it. Written in zero focalization: no character's understanding, no historical context, no narrative deployment. This is true before the story begins and will be true after it ends. If a scene appears to violate what you write here, the story has an internal consistency problem. Do not write what characters believe about this principle — write what is actually true. Do not write how it was discovered — write what it is./ ------------------------------------------------------------------------ *Governing Logic track on Constructed Idea entries* Display question: /How does this constructed idea operate, and what does it produce?/ Tooltip: /Document the rules by which this institution, policy, doctrine, or framework functions — its operating logic, its mechanisms, its outputs given specific conditions, its failure states. Written from outside time as if describing a designed system to an engineer who needs to verify it works as intended. This is distinct from how it was built (Track B) and from what characters think about it (scene payloads). Ask: if I needed to verify that a scene using this institution is internally consistent, what would I check against? Write that./ ------------------------------------------------------------------------ *Binding Logic track on Organization entries* Display question: /What holds this organization's members together and why do they stay?/ Tooltip: /State the principle, shared commitment, mutual interest, or enforcement mechanism that constitutes this organization as a collective entity. This is the answer to: why do members act collectively rather than individually, and what would cause the organization to fracture or dissolve? Write this as structural fact, not as any member's self- perception of their belonging. Members may have wrong beliefs about why they stay — write what is actually true about the cohesive force./ ------------------------------------------------------------------------ *Mechanics track on Technology entries* Display question: /How does this technology physically work?/ Tooltip: /Document the operational specifications of this technology — inputs, outputs, physical constraints, failure conditions, the causal chain from activation to effect. Written as an engineering specification, not a narrative description. A reader of this field should be able to determine whether any scene using this technology is mechanically consistent without needing other context. Do not write what characters think the technology does — write what it actually does. Do not write how it was invented — write how it operates./ ------------------------------------------------------------------------ *Track A: Psychological Architecture on Character entries* Display question: /Who is this character at their core, and why?/ Tooltip: /State this character's psychological architecture — the wound, the belief system built around the wound, the epistemic limits that follow from that belief system, and the specific things they cannot perceive because of it. Written as standing truth that holds from the story's beginning. This is not what the character thinks about themselves — it is the objective structure of their psychology that their behavior expresses without their full awareness. Do not write what happens to them (Track B). Do not write how they should be perceived in any specific scene (scene payloads). Write who they fundamentally are./ ------------------------------------------------------------------------ *Track B: Historical Record on Character and Codex entries* Display question: /What objectively happened, in sequence, that made this what it is?/ Tooltip: /Document the chronological, causal chain of events that produced the current state of this entity. Written as an omniscient historian's account — what occurred, when, because of what prior conditions, with what consequences. No character's perception of events. No narrative deployment decisions. No authorial commentary. Include the WorldDate field on each note where known. If two notes in this track are not causally connected, one of them may belong elsewhere./ ------------------------------------------------------------------------ *Operational Instance track on PlotPointCodexEntry for World Principle* Display question: /What is this World Principle actually doing in this scene?/ Tooltip: /State in zero focalization how this World Principle is operating in this specific scene — what it is producing, what it is constraining, what its presence explains about the scene's events. This is not what any character perceives about it. This is the author's confirmation that the principle is genuinely active in this scene, not merely referenced. If you cannot write a specific operational instance, the World Principle may not actually be present in this scene and should be removed from the connection./ ------------------------------------------------------------------------ *ReaderAccumulation track on PlotPointCodexEntry (all categories)* Display question: /What does the reader now understand about this entity, and what are they still missing?/ Tooltip: /Document the reader's current model of this entity after this scene — what they have been given, what remains undisclosed, and what their partial understanding implies. This is Stage 2 content: written from the reader's epistemic position, not the author's omniscient one. Optional — only populate when this appearance is doing deliberate revelation work toward a planned disclosure. If the entity is merely present or referenced without advancing the reader's understanding, leave this track empty./ ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Unset Queue as a First-Class Feature Given the exclusive track principle, Unset should be visible and scannable as a first-class feature rather than just the default state. A dedicated Unset view showing all notes across all entities that have not been assigned a track, sorted by entity and with their content visible, is the primary data sanitation tool during the migration phase. The workflow during migration is: open the Unset queue, read a note, ask the display question for each candidate track, assign the first track where the answer is an unambiguous yes, split the note if multiple tracks produce a yes. Notes that remain ambiguous after this process stay in Unset with a FlagReason explaining the ambiguity. The flag is not a failure — it is an obligation for future resolution, potentially through further world-building work that clarifies the assertion type. The Unset queue shrinking over time is the primary metric of data sanitation progress. It does not need to reach zero before prose work begins. It needs to reach a state where the notes governing the scenes in your drafting queue are Confirmed in their correct tracks. You said: Do I even need hard labels for the note tracks? Do I even need hard labels for the note tracks? I'm now leaning towards defining an entity configuration for each type where the note track question and reasoning are defined along with an index number. The view model would use the configuration to render Apr 25 Claude responded: Yes. Weighed configuration-driven approach against hard-coded enum labels Weighed configuration-driven approach against hard-coded enum labels Yes. This is the correct architectural decision and it is better than hard labels for several precise reasons. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Why Configuration Beats Hard Labels Hard labels on an enum mean every track type is a compile-time constant. Adding a new track type requires a code change, a migration, and a recompile. Discovering during data entry that an entity type needs a track you did not anticipate — which will happen — becomes a development task rather than a configuration task. A configuration-driven approach means the track definitions live in data. Adding a track type for a new entity configuration is a data entry task. The view model does not know or care what the tracks are — it renders whatever the configuration specifies. The question text, the tooltip, the index, the singleton/multi-note constraint, the required/ optional status, and the track's cognitive mode label are all properties of the configuration record rather than properties of the enum. This also means the configuration itself becomes part of your story planner's self-documentation. Opening the configuration for a Character entity shows you the complete epistemological architecture of what a Character entry is supposed to contain, in the same interface where you work with the content. The configuration is the explicit statement of the framework. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Configuration Record Structure Each track configuration needs these fields to drive rendering correctly: csharp |public class NoteTrackConfiguration { public int Id { get; set; } // Which entity type this track belongs to public EntityType EntityType { get; set; } // Display order within the entity's track list public int Index { get; set; } // Short label shown in the tab or section header // e.g. "Psychological Architecture" or "Governing Logic" public string TrackName { get; set; } // The single question visible during data entry // Under 15 words. The operational test for note assignment. // e.g. "Who is this character at their core, and why?" public string DisplayQuestion { get; set; } // Verbose reference text shown in tooltip or expandable panel public string Tooltip { get; set; } // Whether this track holds one note (singleton) or many public bool IsSingleton { get; set; } // Whether an empty track is a diagnostic signal // Required tracks produce a completion warning when empty public bool IsRequired { get; set; } // The cognitive mode this track requires // Used for visual differentiation and mode-switching cues public CognitiveMode CognitiveMode { get; set; } // Whether notes in this track carry WorldDate public bool SupportsWorldDate { get; set; } // Whether notes in this track use NoteState (Unset/Flagged/Confirmed) // or the softer Reviewed state used for Development Notes public bool UsesSoftConfirmation { get; set; } } public enum CognitiveMode { ZeroFocalization, // Author as historian/physicist SceneArchitecture, // Author as director ProjectManagement, // Author to future self Bibliographic // Author as researcher } public enum EntityType { Character, CodexWorldPrinciple, CodexConstructedIdea, CodexTechnology, CodexOrganization, CodexGeographicReference, Chapter, StoryThread, Theme, PlotPoint, PlotPointCharacter, PlotPointCodexEntry, PlotPointStoryThread, PlotPointTheme }| ------------------------------------------------------------------------ What This Enables The view model queries configurations by EntityType and renders tracks in Index order. Each track section shows TrackName as the header, DisplayQuestion as always-visible helper text, and Tooltip on hover or expand. The CognitiveMode drives a subtle visual distinction — zero focalization tracks might have a neutral color, scene architecture tracks a warmer tone, project management tracks a muted treatment. This visual differentiation reinforces the mode-switching without requiring explicit labels. Required tracks with no Confirmed notes produce a diagnostic indicator on the entity card in list views. The completion profile the view model computes is entirely driven by configuration — which tracks are required, which are singleton, which need Confirmed notes — without any hardcoded logic about specific track types. When you discover during data entry that a Geographic Reference entry needs a track you did not anticipate, you add a configuration record. No code change. The next time you open a Geographic Reference entry, the new track renders. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Unset Queue Integration The Unset queue becomes a configuration-aware view. Notes in Unset have no track assignment. The queue displays them alongside the configuration for their parent entity, showing all available tracks with their display questions. The assignment gesture is: read the note, scan the display questions, tap the correct one. The question drives the decision. The label is incidental. This is the practical payoff of the configuration approach — the display question is not documentation about the system. It is the system. The author never needs to remember what goes in Track A versus Track B because the question is always visible at the point of decision. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ One Practical Constraint The NoteTrackConfiguration table needs to be seeded with sensible defaults before first use. The initial seed covers all the tracks defined in this conversation. After that, additions are data operations. The seed script is effectively the compiled version of everything this conversation has established about what belongs where — which means writing it is the final verification that the framework is internally consistent. If any track's display question cannot be written in under fifteen words without losing its specificity, that is a signal the track definition is not yet precise enough. You said: Can the index also be correlated with a function key which is also displayed? Can the index also be correlated with a function key which is also displayed? Pressing the function key moves the note currently we the cursor to the track. In WPF, are any of the function keys used by default or are all 12 available? There's no hard connection between the index and function key but they'll be made sensible. Like make all the truth tracks F1, historical record F2, Macro architecture F3. This is all configurable and updates the helper UI Apr 25 Claude responded: Function Keys in WPF Function Keys in WPF In WPF, most function keys are available for custom binding. The ones with default behaviors to be aware of: *F1* — by WPF convention opens Help. Not bound at the framework level but many applications and users expect it for help. Worth avoiding or overriding deliberately with awareness. *F2* — no WPF default. Common convention in Windows for rename operations (Explorer, file dialogs). Available but may feel semantically odd for Historical Record if users have strong muscle memory. *F3* — no WPF default. Common convention for Find Next in many applications. Available. *F4* — opens the dropdown on a ComboBox when focused, and Alt+F4 closes the window. F4 alone is available but test carefully that it does not interact unexpectedly with focused controls. *F5* — no WPF default. Common refresh convention in browsers. Available. *F6* through *F12* — no WPF defaults. Fully available. The practical recommendation is to treat F1 as reserved for a help/ tooltip toggle that reveals the full tooltip for the currently focused track, rather than binding it to a content track. This respects the convention and gives you a useful feature. Your content track bindings then start at F2, which aligns naturally with a low index number. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Configuration Extension csharp |public class NoteTrackConfiguration { public int Id { get; set; } public EntityType EntityType { get; set; } public int Index { get; set; } // F1 reserved for help, content tracks start at F2 // Null means no hotkey assigned for this track public int? FunctionKeyNumber { get; set; } public string TrackName { get; set; } public string DisplayQuestion { get; set; } public string Tooltip { get; set; } public bool IsSingleton { get; set; } public bool IsRequired { get; set; } public CognitiveMode CognitiveMode { get; set; } public bool SupportsWorldDate { get; set; } public bool UsesSoftConfirmation { get; set; } // Computed display string shown in the track header // e.g. "F2 — Psychological Architecture" // Null if no function key assigned public string? HotkeyLabel => FunctionKeyNumber.HasValue ? $"F{FunctionKeyNumber} — {TrackName}" : TrackName; }| ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The WPF Binding Register the key bindings at the window or user control level so they are active whenever the entity editor is focused, not just when a specific control has focus: csharp |// In the entity editor view's constructor or Loaded handler private void RegisterTrackHotkeys( IEnumerable configs) { foreach (var config in configs .Where(c => c.FunctionKeyNumber.HasValue)) { var key = KeyFromFunctionNumber( config.FunctionKeyNumber!.Value); var binding = new KeyBinding( new RelayCommand(() => MoveSelectedNoteToTrack(config.Id)), key, ModifierKeys.None); InputBindings.Add(binding); } } private static Key KeyFromFunctionNumber(int n) => n switch { 1 => Key.F1, 2 => Key.F2, 3 => Key.F3, 4 => Key.F4, 5 => Key.F5, 6 => Key.F6, 7 => Key.F7, 8 => Key.F8, 9 => Key.F9, 10 => Key.F10, 11 => Key.F11, 12 => Key.F12, _ => throw new ArgumentOutOfRangeException(nameof(n)) };| The |MoveSelectedNoteToTrack| command on the view model handles the actual assignment and triggers the Unset queue refresh. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Helper UI Display Each track section header renders the hotkey label from the computed property. During an active note selection — when a note is focused or selected in the Unset queue — all track headers highlight their function key badge to signal that pressing that key will assign the note. This gives the author a scannable one-glance view of all available assignments without requiring them to remember the mappings. A small persistent legend strip at the bottom of the editor, visible whenever a note is selected, shows all active function key assignments for the current entity type in a single row. This is the practical equivalent of the tooltip being always-visible at the moment of decision — the author's eye can drop to the strip, read the question associated with each key, and press the correct one without breaking flow. |F1 Help | F2 Psychology | F3 History | F4 Macro Arch | F5 Development | F6 Bibliographic| When the entity type changes — switching from a Character to a Codex entry — the legend strip updates to reflect the new entity's configuration. The function key numbers stay consistent across entity types for the same cognitive mode (F2 always means the primary truth track, F3 always means historical record) which allows muscle memory to develop across entity types rather than requiring relearning per entity. When exporting to markdown for an LLM to understand context should there be a separate text field explaining the note track or can a field do double duty? Apr 25 Claude responded: The display question does double duty cleanly and is the right choice. The display question does double duty cleanly and is the right choice. The display question is already written to be the precise, complete statement of what a track contains — under fifteen words, specific enough to distinguish the track from all others, written in plain language. When it appears as a header in the markdown export, it tells the LLM exactly what kind of assertion the notes beneath it represent. This is more useful than a track name alone ("Track A" or "Psychological Architecture" tells the LLM less than "Who is this character at their core, and why?") and more concise than the full tooltip. The markdown structure becomes: markdown |## Applejack ### Who is this character at their core, and why? Applejack's core wound is suppressed legitimate ambition... ### What objectively happened that made this character who they are? Born to Bright Mac and Pear Butter on Sweet Apple Acres... ### What is the author's plan for how this truth is revealed across the story? Applejack's wound surfaces gradually through behavioral...| The LLM reading this immediately understands the assertion type of each block from the question without needing a separate explanatory field. The question is both the UI helper text for the author and the semantic header for the LLM. One field, two uses, no redundancy. The tooltip does not export. It is authorial documentation about why the track exists and what cognitive mode it requires — useful for the author learning the system, irrelevant to the LLM processing the content. You said: What about the enums for link entities, should these also be driven by configuration? What about the enums for link entities, should these also be driven by configuration? Apr 25 Claude responded: Yes, for the same reason as the track configuration — but the implementation has a meaningful difference worth thinking through before building. Yes, for the same reason as the track configuration — but the implementation has a meaningful difference worth thinking through before building. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Case For Configuration-Driven Enums The UsageType values on PlotPointCodexEntry are the clearest example of the problem. The correct values differ by codex category: a World Principle uses Implied/Observed/Named/Subverted/Demonstrated, a Technology uses Mentioned/ActiveUsage/Definition/Subversion/Failure/ FirstReveal, an Organization uses Represented/Acting Collectively/ Referenced/Structurally Invoked/Fractured. If these are a single flat enum, every codex connection shows all values regardless of which category the connected entry belongs to. The author has to mentally filter out the irrelevant options every time. Configuration-driven enum values mean the dropdown for a PlotPointCodexEntry connection shows only the values appropriate for the connected entry's category. The view model queries the configuration using the connected entity's type and renders only the relevant options. Adding a new value for a specific category is a data operation, not a code change. The same argument applies to GapType on PlotPointCharacter — the values Ironic, Tragic, Closing, Aligned, Paralipsis, Synthesis are not all equally relevant in every scene. Synthesis only applies at the chapter level. Paralipsis requires specific macro architecture conditions. A configuration could suppress irrelevant options based on context. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Meaningful Difference From Track Configuration Track configuration maps one-to-many: one configuration record produces one track section in the UI, containing many notes. The relationship is straightforward. Enum configuration maps to a selection control: one configuration set produces a dropdown or segmented control where exactly one value is selected. The difference matters for the data model. For tracks, the configuration record references the entity type and the view model renders accordingly. For enums, each configuration record is a valid value for a specific context, and the connection entity stores the selected value's identifier rather than the enum integer. This means the stored value needs to be stable — if you reconfigure the enum options, existing stored values must remain valid. The cleanest approach is storing the configuration record's Id as the selected value rather than a position-dependent integer. The display label, tooltip, and contextual filtering are all properties of the configuration record. The stored value is just a foreign key. csharp |public class EnumValueConfiguration { public int Id { get; set; } // Which enum this value belongs to public EnumType EnumType { get; set; } // Which entity type or category this value applies to // Null means applies to all contexts public EntityType? ApplicableEntityType { get; set; } // Display order within the dropdown public int Index { get; set; } // The label shown in the UI public string DisplayLabel { get; set; } // Brief explanation shown as tooltip or helper text // Answers: when should I select this value? public string SelectionGuidance { get; set; } // Whether this value triggers additional required fields // e.g. FirstReveal triggers Physical Presence Note requirement public bool TriggersAdditionalRequirement { get; set; } } public enum EnumType { UsageType, GapType, GoalTrajectory, CharacterRole, CharacterDevImpact, ThemeProminence, NoteState, DraftStatus, TensionPhase, CoreDriver, ConflictType, Presentation }| The connection entity then stores: csharp |public partial class PlotPointCodexEntry { // Replaces the UsageType enum field // Foreign key to EnumValueConfiguration public int? UsageTypeConfigId { get; set; } [JsonIgnore] public EnumValueConfiguration? UsageTypeConfig { get; set; } }| ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Which Enums Benefit Most Not all enums benefit equally from configuration. The decision criterion is whether the correct values genuinely differ by context, or whether the values are universal. *High benefit — context-dependent values:* UsageType on PlotPointCodexEntry — values differ by codex category as described. Clear win for configuration. GapType on PlotPoint and PlotPointCharacter — Synthesis only applies at chapter level, Paralipsis requires specific conditions. Configuration suppresses contextually irrelevant options. *Moderate benefit — universal values but guidance varies:* GoalTrajectory on PlotPointStoryThread — the values themselves are universal but the SelectionGuidance can explain what Disaster means for a specific thread type versus another. Configuration adds tooltip guidance without restricting options. ThemeProminence on PlotPointTheme — similarly universal values where guidance helps more than restriction. CharacterRole and CharacterDevImpact on PlotPointCharacter — universal values. Configuration adds guidance but does not need to restrict by context. *Low benefit — stable technical values:* NoteState, DraftStatus, TensionPhase, CoreDriver, ConflictType, Presentation — these are authorial analysis frameworks that apply uniformly. The values are stable, the contexts do not differ, and configuration adds overhead without meaningful benefit. Keep these as standard enums. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Practical Boundary Configure the enums where context-dependent filtering produces meaningfully better UX — UsageType and GapType are the clear cases. Use standard enums for everything else and add tooltips at the UI layer rather than through configuration records. The configuration table for enums will be small — perhaps thirty to forty records covering UsageType variants across four codex categories and GapType variants across two connection entity types. This is manageable seeding work that produces a meaningful UX improvement at the exact moment of data entry where the author most needs guidance: when assigning a usage type to a connection and wondering which of twelve options applies. Examined making enum types themselves data-driven rather than hardcoded Examined making enum types themselves data-driven rather than hardcoded This is the correct extension and it follows directly from the same principle that drove the track configuration decision. If the track types are data rather than code, the connection property types should be data too. The reasoning is identical: hardcoding the property types means every new analytical axis you discover requires a code change and migration. Making the property types themselves configurable means adding a new connection property is a data operation. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ What This Actually Means Currently the connection entities have discrete enum fields defined in C#: csharp |public partial class PlotPointCharacter { public CharacterRole Role { get; set; } public CharacterDevImpact DevelopmentImpact { get; set; } public GapType ObservedGapType { get; set; } }| Each field is a hardcoded property with a hardcoded enum type. Adding a new analytical axis — say, you decide every PlotPointCharacter connection should also track the character's emotional state at the scene's end — requires adding a new field to the model, a new enum, and a migration. The configuration-driven alternative replaces these discrete fields with a collection of property assignments, where both the property definition and its value set are stored as configuration data: csharp |// The definition of a connection property type public class ConnectionPropertyDefinition { public int Id { get; set; } // Which connection entity type this property applies to public ConnectionEntityType ConnectionEntityType { get; set; } // Display order in the UI public int Index { get; set; } // Optional function key for quick assignment public int? The reasoning is that GapType is structurally load-bearing for the completion signal architecture — the view model uses it to compute scene design completeness, not just to display analytical information. Properties that drive system behavior rather than capture analytical content belong as discrete fields. Properties that purely capture the author's analysis of a connection belong in the configuration system. The same logic applies to GoalTrajectory on PlotPointStoryThread and IsPrimary — these drive filtering and completion logic and belong as discrete fields. ThemeProminence on PlotPointTheme drives the visual badge system and belongs discrete. The clean division is: *behavioral properties stay discrete, analytical properties become configurable.* ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Seeding Reality The initial seed for ConnectionPropertyDefinition covers what were previously hardcoded enums: PlotPointCharacter gets definitions for: Character Role (Protagonist/ Antagonist/Supporting), Development Impact (the CharacterDevImpact values), and the new analytical properties established in this conversation — LogicalOrder remains discrete as a structural field. PlotPointStoryThread gets a definition for ThreadScope visibility if needed beyond the discrete GoalTrajectory. PlotPointCodexEntry gets UsageType as a configurable property with values scoped by the connected codex category's EntityType — this is where the context-dependent filtering discussed earlier is implemented cleanly. The PropertyDefinition for UsageType has a nullable ApplicableEntityType. The view model queries: give me all ConnectionPropertyValues for this PropertyDefinition where ApplicableEntityType matches the connected codex entry's category. The dropdown shows only those values. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The UX Consequence The connection entity editor becomes a unified panel driven entirely by configuration. When you open a PlotPointCharacter connection, the view model queries all ConnectionPropertyDefinitions for PlotPointCharacter, renders each as a labeled dropdown with its DisplayQuestion visible and SelectionGuidance in the tooltip, and displays the current assignment or an empty state. The note tracks render below, also from configuration. The entire analytical surface of a connection entity — what properties it has, what values are valid, what guidance is shown, which function keys assign values — is visible in the configuration tables and editable without touching code. When you decide three chapters into drafting that every PlotPointCharacter connection should also track whether the character is aware of the scene's central irony, you add a PropertyDefinition and its values, and every PlotPointCharacter editor in the application immediately shows the new property. The function key integration extends naturally: PropertyDefinitions have FunctionKeyNumber just as NoteTrackConfigurations do. Within the connection editor, pressing the assigned key opens the dropdown for that property. The persistent legend strip at the bottom of the editor shows both track hotkeys and property hotkeys in a single scannable row, reflecting the full analytical surface of the current connection entity type. become StoryEntity rows with the appropriate StoryEntityConfigurationId. The migration is mechanical. No content is lost and no new tables are added when you later decide you need a new entity type. *One view model.* StoryEntityViewModel renders any entity type by querying its configuration and building the track list dynamically. The window does not know whether it is showing a Character or a World Principle. It knows it is showing a StoryEntity with a specific configuration, and it renders accordingly. *One link type.* PlotPointEntityLink replaces PlotPointCharacter, PlotPointCodexEntry, PlotPointStoryThread, and PlotPointTheme. The PlotPointLinkConfigurationId determines what kind of link it is and therefore what properties and note tracks to render. The discrete fields that remain — LogicalOrder, IsPrimary, GoalTrajectory, ObservedGapType, ThemeProminence — cover all the behavioral properties needed across all link types. A link that does not use a given discrete field leaves it at its default value. This is a small amount of sparse data, not a normalization problem. *Configuration drives everything visible.* The Unset queue, the completion diagnostics, the function key legend, the export filter — all query configuration to know what to render and what to check. None of it has hardcoded knowledge of entity or link types. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The One Genuine Risk Sparse discrete fields on PlotPointEntityLink mean a Character link carries GoalTrajectory and ThemeProminence columns that are meaningless for characters, and a Theme link carries ObservedGapType that is meaningless for themes. This is acceptable because these are behavioral properties that drive system logic — they are not user-facing analytical fields — and the configuration determines which ones are relevant and therefore visible for each link type. The author never sees GoalTrajectory on a character link because the PlotPointLinkConfiguration for characters does not render it. If this feels wrong, the alternative is moving all discrete behavioral properties into ConnectionPropertyAssignment as well, with special handling in the view model for the ones that drive system logic. This is cleaner in schema terms but adds indirection to the completion signal architecture. The sparse discrete field approach is the pragmatic choice for a solo project where the schema will not be inspected by anyone who does not know why the columns are there. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The ViewModel Structure csharp |public class StoryEntityViewModel : ObservableObject { public StoryEntity Entity { get; } public StoryEntityConfiguration Configuration { get; } // Built from NoteTrackConfiguration for this entity type public ObservableCollection Tracks { get; } // Completion profile derived from configuration requirements public EntityCompletionProfile CompletionProfile { get; } } public class PlotPointEntityLinkViewModel : ObservableObject { public PlotPointEntityLink Link { get; } public PlotPointLinkConfiguration LinkConfiguration { get; } // Built from ConnectionPropertyDefinition for this link config public ObservableCollection Properties { get; } // Built from NoteTrackConfiguration for this link config public ObservableCollection Tracks { get; } // Discrete behavioral properties exposed directly // for binding to specific controls public GapType ObservedGapType { get; set; } public GoalTrajectory GoalTrajectory { get; set; } public bool IsPrimary { get; set; } public ThemeProminence ThemeProminence { get; set; } } public class NoteTrackViewModel : ObservableObject { public NoteTrackConfiguration Configuration { get; } // The display question — used in UI and markdown export public string DisplayQuestion => Configuration.DisplayQuestion; // Hotkey label for legend strip public string? The question is not just whether a property is required but *what work block its completion belongs to and what it gates*. GoalTrajectory on a StoryThread link answers a question that must be answered during Macro Architecture work. An empty GoalTrajectory on a thread connection signals that the author has not yet decided how this scene moves the thread. This is a Stage 1 validation gap — the structural record is incomplete. GapType on a character connection answers a question that belongs to Scene Architecture work. An empty GapType does not signal that Stage 1 is incomplete. It signals that Stage 2 has not been done for this character in this scene. This is a Stage 2 validation gap — the experience design is incomplete. A third category exists: properties that are informational annotations with no validation consequence. LogicalOrder is an example — it organizes display but its absence does not signal incomplete work at any stage. csharp |public class ConnectionPropertyDefinition { public int Id { get; set; } public int PlotPointLinkConfigurationId { get; set; } public int Index { get; set; } public int? FunctionKeyNumber { get; set; } public string DisplayName { get; set; } public string DisplayQuestion { get; set; } public string Tooltip { get; set; } // What work block does completing this property belong to? // Drives which completion profile this property contributes to public ValidationTier ValidationTier { get; set; } // Whether absence of a value is a diagnostic signal // at the specified tier public bool IsValidationRequired { get; set; } // Guidance shown when the property is empty and required // at its tier — explains what work needs to happen public string? EmptyStateGuidance { get; set; } public bool HasConditionalRequirements { get; set; } } public enum ValidationTier { // No validation consequence — informational only None = 0, // Required for Stage 1 completeness // Structural record is incomplete without this StructuralRecord = 1, // Required for Stage 2 completeness // Experience design is incomplete without this ExperienceDesign = 2, // Required for Stage 3 completeness // Delivery blueprint is incomplete without this SceneBlueprint = 3 }| ------------------------------------------------------------------------ How Existing Properties Map *GoalTrajectory on StoryThread link* ValidationTier: StructuralRecord IsValidationRequired: true EmptyStateGuidance: "Assign a trajectory before this scene's thread record is complete. How does this scene move the thread — positive, negative, disaster, triumph, or stagnant?" *IsPrimary on StoryThread link* ValidationTier: None IsValidationRequired: false Informational — affects display badge but absence signals nothing about work stage. *GapType on character link* ValidationTier: ExperienceDesign IsValidationRequired: false initially — you can promote it to true once you understand how it integrates with your workflow. The tier is set correctly so when you do enable validation, it contributes to the Stage 2 completion profile rather than Stage 1. *ThemeProminence on theme link* ValidationTier: StructuralRecord IsValidationRequired: true The prominence level is part of the objective record of what the theme is doing in this scene. *CharacterRole on character link* ValidationTier: StructuralRecord IsValidationRequired: true Whether a character is protagonist, antagonist, or supporting in a scene is a structural fact needed before experience design begins. *CharacterDevImpact on character link* ValidationTier: StructuralRecord IsValidationRequired: false Valuable when populated, but absence does not necessarily mean the structural record is incomplete — not every character appearance involves development impact. *LogicalOrder on all link types* ValidationTier: None IsValidationRequired: false ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Completion Profile Computation The PlotPoint completion profile now reads directly from ValidationTier across all its links and their note tracks: csharp |public class PlotPointCompletionProfile { // Stage 1: all StructuralRecord tier properties and note tracks // on all connections are populated/confirmed public bool IsStage1Complete { get; } // Stage 2: all ExperienceDesign tier properties and note tracks // on all connections are populated/confirmed // AND Stage 1 is complete public bool IsStage2Complete { get; } // Stage 3: Synopsis track has confirmed notes // AND Stage 2 is complete public bool IsStage3Complete { get; } // Per-connection breakdown for diagnostic display public IReadOnlyList LinkStatuses { get; } } public class LinkCompletionStatus { public PlotPointEntityLink Link { get; } // Which required properties at each tier are empty public IReadOnlyList MissingStage1Properties { get; } public IReadOnlyList MissingStage2Properties { get; } // Which required note tracks at each tier are empty public IReadOnlyList MissingStage1Tracks { get; } public IReadOnlyList MissingStage2Tracks { get; } }| ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Practical Consequence for GapType You configure GapType with ValidationTier: ExperienceDesign and IsValidationRequired: false. It appears in the connection editor with its display question and tooltip. Populating it is encouraged but absence produces no warning. When your data has matured enough that you understand how GapType integrates with your scene design workflow — when you have populated it for enough scenes to develop intuition about when it is genuinely useful versus when it is overhead — you update the configuration record: IsValidationRequired: true. From that point forward, every PlotPointCharacter link with an empty GapType shows as incomplete in the Stage 2 completion profile. No code change. No migration. One data update to the configuration record cascades through the entire completion signal architecture. This is the practical payoff of the configuration-driven approach: properties that you are uncertain about can exist in the system at ValidationTier: None or with IsValidationRequired: false, contributing to the UI and the export without gatekeeping workflow. When confidence develops through contact with real data, the validation can be tightened through configuration. The architecture accommodates the author's growing understanding without requiring the author to have complete understanding before building. You said: What if I used "Subject" instead of "StoryEntity"? What if I used "Subject" instead of "StoryEntity"? And "PlotPointSubjectLink" Apr 25 Claude responded: Subject is better than StoryEntity for several precise reasons. Subject is better than StoryEntity for several precise reasons. *StoryEntity* is a technical term that describes what the table is in the system — an entity in the story planner's data model. It is named from the software's perspective rather than the author's perspective. Every time you read it during data entry you are reminded you are interacting with a database abstraction rather than a story element. *Subject* is the term a developmental editor or story analyst would actually use. "What is the subject of this scene?" "Which subjects does this plot point touch?" "Open the Chrysalis subject." It is the word that naturally fits the cognitive mode of story design rather than software engineering. A Character is a subject. A World Principle is a subject. An Organization is a subject. The word works without friction across all entity configurations. Subject also has a precise literary meaning that aligns with your usage — the subject of a narrative is what the narrative is about at any given moment. A PlotPoint has subjects: the characters, codex entries, themes, and threads it engages with. The word carries this meaning without needing explanation. *PlotPointSubjectLink* follows cleanly. It reads as "the link between a plot point and one of its subjects" which is exactly what it is. The word Link is similarly clean — it names the relationship without implying anything about its content. The only minor consideration is that Subject can also mean a person under a ruler's authority — "Celestia's subjects" — which has in- universe meaning in TLTT. This is unlikely to cause genuine confusion since the data model context makes the meaning clear, but it is worth noting. The full table naming then becomes: sql |Subject -- was StoryEntity SubjectConfiguration -- was StoryEntityConfiguration PlotPointSubjectLink -- was PlotPointEntityLink PlotPointSubjectLinkConfiguration -- was PlotPointLinkConfiguration| These names read naturally in queries and in the UI. "Show all Subjects of type Character" and "Show all PlotPointSubjectLinks for this PlotPoint" are sentences a story designer would write without needing to translate from software vocabulary.
