Show more Apr 25 Claude responded: Analysis of the Architecture Analysis of the Architecture The plan is correct and well-reasoned for your specific constraints. Each decision follows from the single-user, load-everything-at-startup, desktop application nature of the project. No Navigation Properties Removing navigation properties from the model classes is the right call here. Navigation properties exist primarily to support lazy loading, eager loading with Include, and relationship traversal in LINQ queries that execute against the database. When everything is already in memory, these mechanisms are overhead without benefit. EF Core navigation properties also carry subtle risks in a desktop application — accidental lazy loading after the context window has moved, change tracking surprises when the same entity is referenced through multiple navigation paths, and serialization complications with circular references that you are already handling with JsonIgnore attributes. Using the model classes purely as change tracking vessels with Id fields only is clean. The view models do the relationship work using filtered views on the in-memory collections. This separation is honest about what each layer is doing: the model classes are database row representations, the view models are the application's working data structures. The ICollectionView filtering approach is exactly right for this architecture. ObservableCollection tied to DbSet.Local gives you the EF change tracking for free while the ICollectionView gives you efficient filtered projections without duplicating data. A SubjectViewModel filtering PlotPointSubjectLink on SubjectId is doing what a SQL join would do, but in memory where it is cheap and already loaded. OwnerId as Polymorphic Foreign Key The Note having OwnerId rather than separate nullable foreign key columns per owner type is a deliberate tradeoff. The previous model had separate nullable columns — ChapterId, CharacterId, CodexEntryId, ThemeId, etc. — which is the standard relational approach for polymorphic associations. Collapsing this to OwnerId plus an OwnerType discriminator is cleaner for your use case because the filtering logic in the view model is uniform: filter notes where OwnerId matches this entity's Id and NoteTrackDefinitionId matches the desired track. The owner type discriminator tells the view model which collection to look in when navigating from a Note back to its owner, which you will occasionally need. The risk with polymorphic foreign keys in a relational database is that you lose referential integrity enforcement at the database level — the database cannot enforce that OwnerId actually references a valid row because it does not know which table to check. For a single-user desktop application that controls all data entry through a typed view model layer, this is an acceptable tradeoff. The application layer enforces the integrity that the database would otherwise enforce. Metadata in Tables This is the correct and complete description of the paradigm. The general term is *Entity-Attribute-Value* (EAV) when taken to its extreme, but your architecture is more precisely described as *metadata- driven design* or *table-driven configuration*. A closely related term from enterprise software is *soft-coding* as opposed to hard-coding — moving decisions that would normally be encoded in the schema or the application code into rows in configuration tables. The specific variant you have implemented is sometimes called a *type- table pattern* or *category-table pattern* — where SubjectDefinition is not a type in the C# sense but a row in a table that configures the behavior of Subject instances associated with it. NoteTrackDefinition configures what tracks exist. NarrativePropertyDefinition configures what analytical properties exist. The shape of the data is stored as data rather than as schema. The reason this is distinct from pure EAV is that you still have typed tables for the primary content — Subject, PlotPoint, PlotPointSubjectLink, Chapter, Note all have fixed schemas. Only the configuration layer is table-driven. This is the healthy middle ground: the content benefits from relational integrity and typed columns, while the configuration benefits from flexibility and runtime extensibility. Why Relational Database Still Makes Sense Several properties of your data favor relational over document: *Cross-entity queries are your primary access pattern.* The Unset queue query, the completion profile computation, the chronological scan of a Subject's appearances across PlotPoints — these all require joining or filtering across multiple entity types simultaneously. A relational database handles these naturally. A document database requires either denormalization (duplicating data across documents) or application-side joins (loading multiple document collections and joining in memory, which you are already doing anyway). *Change tracking is semantically important.* EF Core's change tracking gives you a transactional unit of work — you can make multiple changes across multiple entity types and commit them atomically, or roll back if something goes wrong. Document databases can approximate this but it is not their natural model. *The configuration tables have genuine relational structure.* NarrativePropertyDefinition belongs to a PlotPointSubjectLinkConfiguration which belongs to a SubjectDefinition. NoteTrackDefinition belongs to either a SubjectDefinition or a PlotPointSubjectLinkConfiguration. NarrativePropertyValueDefinition belongs to a NarrativePropertyDefinition. These relationships have referential integrity that benefits from foreign key constraints. A document database would require you to enforce these relationships in application code. *Your data is not document-shaped.* A document database is natural when each entity is a self-contained unit with deeply nested structure that is always accessed as a whole. Your entities are shallow and highly connected — a Note without its NoteTrackDefinition and its owner entity is meaningless. The connections are the content. Where a Document Database Would Be Better The one area where document storage has genuine appeal is the Note.Content field itself and potentially the longer text fields on Subject and PlotPoint. These are unstructured strings that could benefit from rich text representation, embedded formatting, or future extensibility without schema migration. Some SQLite-based applications handle this by storing JSON in a text column for specific fields while keeping the rest of the schema relational — a hybrid that gives you document flexibility where you need it without abandoning relational integrity where it matters. For your use case this is probably not worth the added complexity. SQLite handles text fields efficiently and your content strings are not large enough to create performance concerns. The pure relational approach with SQLite remains the right choice. The Paradigm's Tradeoff Named Precisely The cost of metadata-driven design is that queries become harder to read and reason about. A query that filters Notes by NoteTrackDefinitionId is less self-documenting than a query that filters Notes where IsOntologyTrack is true. Debugging requires knowing what the configuration data says, not just what the schema says. For a solo developer who built the system and understands the configuration, this cost is low. For a team, it would require documentation investment. The benefit is exactly what you have been pursuing throughout this conversation: the shape of the story planner can evolve through data entry rather than code changes. Adding a new Subject type, a new note track, a new narrative property — these are all configuration operations. The application's behavior changes without the application changing. For a project whose design framework has been evolving rapidly through insight and analysis, this flexibility is not a luxury. It is the architectural decision that makes the system serve the project rather than constrain it. You said: I have uploaded my new work in progress model classes to the project files. Apr 25 Claude responded: The Composition Solution Is Already Present Analyzed composition patterns and concluded OwnerViewModel already solves problem Analyzed composition patterns and concluded OwnerViewModel already solves problem The Composition Solution Is Already Present The OwnerViewModel is the composition. The shared behavior — building NoteTrackViewModels and NarrativePropertyViewModels from definition lists — already lives there. Every view model that needs this behavior inherits OwnerViewModel and calls InitializeCollections. This is correct and should not change. The model layer does not need shared behavior because the model classes are pure data vessels. They do not do anything with their tracks — the view model does. Putting shared behavior on model classes would be giving responsibilities to a layer that should have none. The real structural question is narrower: *how does each entity type expose its DefinitionId so the view model knows which NoteTrackDefinitions to load?* ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Current Architecture Already Solves It NoteTrackDefinition already has OwnerId and OwnerType. This means NoteTrackDefinitions know which entity they belong to, not the other way around. The view model does not ask "give me this Subject's NoteTrackDefinitions via navigation property." It asks the in-memory collection: "give me all NoteTrackDefinitions where OwnerId matches and OwnerType matches." This is the filtered ICollectionView pattern you described. For Subject this currently routes through SubjectDefinition — the Subject has a SubjectDefinitionId, multiple Subjects of the same type share one SubjectDefinition, and NoteTrackDefinitions belong to the SubjectDefinition. This indirection exists because Subject is the only entity type with multiple configurations — a Character Subject and a World Principle Subject need different track layouts, so the definition is a shared template. For PlotPoint, Chapter, and PlotPointSubjectLink there is only one configuration each. They do not need a definition lookup table. Their NoteTrackDefinitions can belong to them directly via OwnerId and OwnerType, which is already how NoteTrackDefinition is structured. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ What Needs to Change PlotPointSubjectLink currently has navigation properties — List and List directly on the model class. This contradicts the no-navigation-properties decision and should be removed. The PlotPointSubjectLinkViewModel will filter the in-memory NoteTrackDefinition collection using OwnerId and OwnerType exactly as SubjectViewModel does. The OwnerType enum already names all four entity types. The filtering pattern is already established. Nothing new needs to be invented. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The INoteable Interface as the Composition Contract INoteable is the correct abstraction at the model layer. Its sole purpose is to expose OwnerType so the view model knows which discriminator to use when filtering the in-memory collections. This is composition through interface rather than inheritance — each entity type implements the contract independently without sharing implementation. The interface could be slightly extended without becoming a base class: csharp |public interface INoteable { int Id { get; } OwnerType OwnerType { get; } }| This gives the view model everything it needs to filter NoteTrackDefinitions, NarrativePropertyDefinitions, and Notes from their respective in-memory collections. The OwnerViewModel receives an INoteable and filters accordingly. No base class, no navigation properties, no inheritance hierarchy in the model layer. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Paradigm Name What you have built is the *Type Object pattern* combined with *polymorphic associations*. The Type Object pattern is the formal name for SubjectDefinition's role — it is a runtime object that defines the structure of instances rather than baking that structure into C# class definitions. A Character Subject and a World Principle Subject are the same C# class with different SubjectDefinition instances. The definition is the type. This is the pattern Martin Fowler documents in Patterns of Enterprise Application Architecture. The polymorphic association is OwnerId plus OwnerType — a single foreign-key-like column that can reference rows in multiple tables depending on the discriminator. This is sometimes called a polymorphic belongs-to in Rails terminology. It trades database-level referential integrity for flexibility, which is the correct tradeoff here because the integrity is enforced by the view model layer. The combination — type objects for template configuration, polymorphic associations for content ownership — is precisely what you need and is already substantially in place. The remaining work is removing the navigation properties from PlotPointSubjectLink and ensuring the view model layer uses ICollectionView filtering uniformly across all four entity types. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Why Not Inheritance Inheritance in EF Core for this scenario would produce either Table Per Hierarchy — one large table with many nullable columns and a discriminator — or Table Per Type — separate tables joined at query time. Both approaches add complexity that serves no benefit when the shared behavior is already correctly placed in the view model layer. More fundamentally, the four entity types are not variations of a common concept at the model level. Subject, PlotPoint, Chapter, and PlotPointSubjectLink have genuinely different structural roles. The fact that they all happen to be owned by NoteTrackDefinitions via the same polymorphic pattern is a storage convenience, not evidence of a conceptual hierarchy. Modeling a storage convenience as inheritance produces a class hierarchy that misrepresents the domain. The current design correctly keeps the model classes flat and independent, with the shared behavior composed at the view model level where it actually belongs. OwnerId is unused or zero. There is conceptually one configuration for the whole type. *Typed entities* (Subject, PlotPointSubjectLink) — OwnerType plus OwnerId is the lookup key. OwnerId references the Definition object. Multiple configurations exist, one per type. NoteTrackDefinition and NarrativePropertyDefinition already support both patterns because they carry both OwnerId and OwnerType. The schema requires no change. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Admin UI Architecture The admin UI manages four definition categories with two behaviors: *PlotPoint tracks* — no definition object to select. Just add a NoteTrackDefinition with OwnerType.PlotPoint. One global list. *Chapter tracks* — same pattern. One global list. *Subject type tracks* — requires selecting which SubjectDefinition to add the track to. The UI shows a dropdown of existing subject types. The track is created with OwnerType.Subject and OwnerId = selected SubjectDefinition.Id. *Link tracks* — requires selecting which PlotPointSubjectLinkDefinition to add the track to. The UI shows a dropdown that displays link definitions by their associated subject type name — "Character Link," "World Principle Link," etc. The track is created with OwnerType.PlotPointSubjectLink and OwnerId = selected PlotPointSubjectLinkDefinition.Id. *Creating a new Subject type* is the one operation that must be atomic across two tables. Creating a new SubjectDefinition must simultaneously create a corresponding PlotPointSubjectLinkDefinition. The UI enforces this by treating it as a single operation: "Create New Subject Type" creates both records. The link definition starts with no tracks — the author then adds link-specific tracks separately. This means the IStoryService gains: csharp |ObservableCollection PlotPointSubjectLinkDefinitions { get; }| And the service's CreateSubjectType operation handles both insertions in a single transaction. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Whether the Divergence Needs Further Unification It does not. The two patterns — OwnerType-only for fixed types, OwnerType-plus-DefinitionId for typed entities — are the correct representation of the structural difference. Forcing PlotPoint and Chapter to have a dummy definition object just to make the lookup pattern uniform would add a table row that has no semantic meaning and would require the view model to do a lookup that always returns the same result. The admin UI is where uniformity matters most, and the four-panel approach above provides a consistent authoring experience without requiring the model layer to pretend that PlotPoint has multiple configuration types it does not have. Remove them everywhere. The friction you identified — models navigating and view models also navigating, creating two paths to the same data with inconsistent behavior — is the direct consequence of mixing the two paradigms. Navigation properties on models imply that the model layer is responsible for relationship traversal. ICollectionView filtering in view models implies that the view model layer is responsible. Both cannot be true simultaneously without producing the confusion you have been experiencing. The clean rule is: *model classes are rows, view models are relationships*. A model class knows its own Id and its foreign key Ids. It knows nothing about the objects those foreign keys point to. All relationship traversal — finding which notes belong to this subject, finding which note tracks apply to this plot point, finding the subject definition for a link — happens in the view model layer using filtered views on the in-memory collections. The complete list of navigation properties to remove: Subject — remove SubjectDefinition navigation property, keep SubjectDefinitionId int field PlotPoint — remove Chapter navigation property, keep ChapterId nullable int field PlotPointSubjectLink — remove NoteTrackDefinitions list, remove NarrativePropertyDefinitions list Chapter — remove PlotPoints collection Note — remove all navigation properties (CodexEntry, Character, Theme, Chapter, StoryThread, SourceMaterial), keep all foreign key Id fields Any remaining navigation property on any model class is a violation of the pattern and should be removed. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ What Changes in OwnerViewModel InitializeCollections currently receives lists. It can now receive the owner's Id and OwnerType directly, and the base class can do the filtering itself if IStoryService is available: csharp |protected void InitializeCollections( int ownerId, OwnerType ownerType, List noteTrackDefinitions, List narrativePropertyDefinitions) { foreach (var ntd in noteTrackDefinitions) { var trackVm = new NoteTrackViewModel( ntd, ownerId, // passed through so NoteTrackViewModel ownerType, // can filter notes correctly _viewModelRegistry, _storyService); NoteTracks.Add(trackVm); } foreach (var npd in narrativePropertyDefinitions) { var propVm = new NarrativePropertyViewModel( npd, ownerId, ownerType, _viewModelRegistry, _storyService); NarrativeProperties.Add(propVm); } }| NoteTrackViewModel holds ownerId and ownerType and uses them to build its filtered note view: csharp |public class NoteTrackViewModel : ObservableObject { private readonly int _ownerId; private readonly OwnerType _ownerType; private readonly NoteTrackDefinition _definition; private readonly IStoryService _storyService; public ICollectionView Notes { get; } public NoteTrackViewModel( NoteTrackDefinition definition, int ownerId, OwnerType ownerType, IViewModelRegistry registry, IStoryService storyService) { _definition = definition; _ownerId = ownerId; _ownerType = ownerType; _storyService = storyService; Notes = CollectionViewSource.GetDefaultView( storyService.Notes); Notes.Filter = FilterNote; } private bool FilterNote(object obj) { if (obj is not Note note) return false; return note.OwnerId == _ownerId && note.OwnerType == _ownerType && note.NoteTrackDefinitionId == _definition.Id; } public string DisplayQuestion => _definition.DisplayQuestion; public string TrackName => _definition.TrackName; public int? FunctionKeyNumber => _definition.FunctionKeyNumber; }| This is the complete elimination of the dual-navigation friction. The model layer has no awareness of relationships. The view model layer constructs all relationships through filtered views on collections that are already in memory. Every access pattern is consistent and there is only one path to any piece of data. Apr 25 Claude responded: Save Immediately on Creation Deciphered ID generation timing dilemma for entity persistence Deciphered ID generation timing dilemma for entity persistence Save Immediately on Creation Save immediately and let EF Core assign the Id. This is the correct approach for your architecture. When you call |context.Add(entity)| and then |context.SaveChanges()|, EF Core inserts the row and populates the entity's Id property with the database-generated value. SQLite uses autoincrement by default for integer primary keys. The entity in memory gets its Id updated in place — the same object reference you are holding now has a valid Id after the save returns. You can immediately use that Id to associate notes, links, and other related records. csharp |public async Task CreateSubjectAsync(int subjectDefinitionId, string name) { var subject = new Subject { SubjectDefinitionId = subjectDefinitionId, Name = name }; _context.Subjects.Add(subject); await _context.SaveChangesAsync(); // subject.Id is now populated by the database // Safe to add to the in-memory ObservableCollection Subjects.Add(subject); return subject; }| ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Why Not Pick the Next Highest Id Manually assigning Ids by finding the current maximum and adding one is fragile and unnecessary. The problems are specific: The ObservableCollection and the database can diverge. If your in-memory collection has an entity that was created but not yet saved, its manually assigned Id is provisional. Any code that filters on Id before the save completes is working with an Id that may not match what the database eventually assigns. Concurrent writes are not an issue for a single-user desktop app, but a logic error in Id assignment is. If you calculate max Id as 47 and assign 48, then a save fails and you retry, you may reassign 48 to a different object. SQLite's autoincrement is reliable and correct. There is no reason to replicate it in application code. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Observable Collection Timing The pattern that works cleanly in your architecture is: create the entity, save immediately, then add to the ObservableCollection. The collection only ever contains entities with valid database Ids. Any view model created from a collection member can immediately use its Id for filtering. csharp |// In IStoryService implementation public async Task CreateSubjectAsync(int subjectDefinitionId, string name) { var subject = new Subject { SubjectDefinitionId = subjectDefinitionId, Name = name }; _context.Subjects.Add(subject); await _context.SaveChangesAsync(); // Id is now valid Subjects.Add(subject); // UI updates via ObservableCollection notification // Any NoteTrackViewModel filtering on this subject's Id // will work correctly immediately }| The alternative — adding to the collection before saving and using a temporary Id — creates a window where the UI can interact with an entity whose Id is not yet valid. In a load-everything architecture where filtering is done by Id, this window produces silent failures: notes filtered by a temporary Id find nothing, or worse, find the wrong notes if the temporary Id happens to collide. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Saving Is Cheap Here In a load-everything-at-startup SQLite application, individual saves for entity creation are not a performance concern. The cost of a single insert in SQLite is microseconds. The save-on-create pattern is safe, simple, and consistent with your architecture's assumptions. Batch saves and deferred Id assignment add complexity without benefit in this context. I need an idea of what will serve me best May 1 Claude responded: What You Are Actually Trying to Do What You Are Actually Trying to Do Before recommending UI, the purpose needs to be stated precisely because the old UI was built for a different purpose than what the system now requires. The old UI treated the payload as *content storage* — you had a thing, and you wrote notes about it. The junction table was a place to put text that belonged to the relationship between two things. The new system treats the payload as *staged design work across distinct cognitive modes*. A PlotPointSubjectLink is not just "notes about this character in this scene." It is a structured design workspace containing zero-focalization objective record, scene architecture specifications, and project management content — each in separate tracks, each answering a distinct question, each feeding a different downstream work block. This changes what the UI needs to do fundamentally. The old UI needed to show you content. The new UI needs to support cognitive mode switching — helping you move between being a historian, a director, and a project manager on the same object without losing context. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Core UX Problem With the Old Paradigm The old approach — subject window with sidebar of linked plot points, plot point window with cards for subjects — created two separate views of the same relationship from opposite ends. You could never see the full link object in one place. Editing the payload required being in either the subject window or the plot point window but never a dedicated context for the link itself. This worked when the link had one text field. It breaks completely when the link has configurable note tracks, narrative property assignments, and a completion profile. A character appearance with six distinct note tracks cannot be adequately represented as a collapsible text box in a sidebar. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Three-Panel Scene Design Workspace The mental model that fits your new system is a *scene design workspace* rather than an entity window. When you open a plot point, you are entering a workspace whose primary purpose is designing one scene. The workspace has three zones: *Left panel — Scene Context.* The plot point's own tracks: Stakes, ReaderState, POVBelief, POVReaderPerception, FIDAnchors, Synopsis, Outcome. The 5 axis enums. DraftStatus. Chapter and order. This is the scene's identity and its top-level design specifications. It is the context that all the link work feeds into. *Center panel — Subject Links.* A list of the PlotPointSubjectLinks for this scene. Each link is represented by a card showing the Subject's name, type badge, the completion profile (stage 1/2/3 indicators), and the values of any narrative properties that have been assigned. The cards are ordered by LogicalOrder. This is the primary working area — you will spend most time here. *Right panel — Active Link Workspace.* When you select a link card in the center panel, the right panel opens the full link as a first-class workspace: all its note tracks in tabs or sections, its narrative property dropdowns, its completion diagnostics. This is where the actual design work for one subject's appearance in this scene happens. The three panels stay open simultaneously. You can see the scene context while working on a specific link. You can see all links while editing one. This supports the cognitive task: you are designing one scene, and each link is a component of that design that must cohere with the others and with the top-level scene specifications. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Subject Window Becomes a Longitudinal View The subject window's purpose changes entirely under this architecture. It is no longer a place to edit the subject's relationship to plot points. It becomes a *longitudinal scan tool* — a read-optimized view of the subject's presence across the whole story. The subject window shows: *Top section* — the subject's own tracks (Track A psychology, Track B history, Track C bibliographic, Project Management). This is the subject's standing truth, independent of any plot point. *Bottom section* — a chronological list of every PlotPointSubjectLink for this subject, ordered by chapter and position within chapter. Each link shows in a compact summary card: the plot point title, the chapter, the ArcStatement note (the single most important Stage 1 field for the chronological scan), the NarrativeProperty values relevant for scanning (GoalTrajectory, ObservedGapType once populated, CharacterRole). This is the arc scan — you scroll through it to verify arc continuity, identify missing ArcStatement notes, and see the rhythm of gap types over time. Clicking a link card in the subject window opens the plot point's full scene design workspace with that link pre-selected in the center panel. Navigation flows from longitudinal scan to scene design, not from scene design back to a subject window. The subject window is for review, not for editing links. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Why This Direction Specifically The key insight driving this recommendation is that your two primary cognitive tasks require opposite information organization: *Scene design* requires breadth — you need all subjects visible simultaneously so you can see how they interact in the scene, verify that the link payloads cohere with each other and with the scene-level specifications, and identify missing design work. The three-panel layout serves this. *Arc review* requires depth — you need to see one subject across all scenes, in order, to verify arc continuity and gap pattern consistency. The longitudinal subject window serves this. The old UI tried to serve both from one layout and served neither well. The subject window with a sidebar of linked plot points was a weak arc review tool because you could only see one payload at a time and could not see scene context. The plot point window with subject cards was a weak scene design tool because the payload was hidden behind a collapse and the link was not a first-class workspace. Separating the two cognitive tasks into two distinct UI modes with navigation between them is the change that will stop the iteration problem. Each mode serves one task well. The navigation between them is directional and clear: scene design workspace for composing, subject window for reviewing. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Additional Practical Recommendations *Completion profile as the primary visual affordance on link cards.* Each link card in the center panel shows a row of small indicators — S1, S2, S3 — colored by completion state. Red means required content is missing at that stage. Yellow means content exists but nothing is Confirmed. Green means the stage is complete. This gives you a dashboard of scene design progress without opening any link. You can scan ten link cards and immediately see which links still need stage 1 work before you can begin stage 2. *Function key bindings active in the right panel.* When the active link workspace is focused, the function key bindings for note track assignment should be live. You are frequently in a flow of reviewing notes and assigning them to tracks. The function keys eliminate the need to reach for the mouse during this flow. *The Unset queue as a global view.* Separate from the scene design workspace and the subject window, the Unset queue is a dedicated global panel showing all notes across all entities and links that have NoteState.Unset, filterable by entity type and cognitive mode. This is the data sanitation workspace — you open it specifically to do classification work, separate from scene design work or arc review work. It has its own function key bindings for track assignment that apply to whatever entity type the selected note belongs to. *Chapter view as the navigation entry point.* The top-level navigation is the chapter list. Opening a chapter shows its plot points as cards with their completion profiles. Opening a plot point card opens the scene design workspace. This creates a natural top-to-bottom navigation flow: story → chapter → scene → link, mirroring the way you would read the story. The subject window is accessible from the navigation sidebar as a parallel view, not as a child of any scene. You said: I like the idea that clicking on a link card gives the full view of that link entity in the same window. I like the idea that clicking on a link card gives the full view of that link entity in the same window. I think subject window should also have the full link control with note tracks available too, so that link note tracks can be populated. Information starts as layer 1-3 content in the subject and needs to be put into the layer 4 tracks of the link for macro architecture before going into micro architecture layer 4 on the scene design level. The OwnerControl will have a top section with data templates specific to the nature of the subclass, like Character, PlotPoint, Character to PlotPoint link each have different requirements for the top bar specific to its design needs. Give an analysis of this approach, both strengths and possible improvements and pitfalls Show more May 1 Claude responded: Analysis of the Unified Content Window Analysis of the Unified Content Window What This Gets Right The three-panel layout with F1-F6 for owner tracks and F7-F12 for link tracks is the most important decision in this design and it is correct. The function key split is not just a convenience — it encodes the cognitive mode boundary between the entity's standing truth and the scene-specific design work. When your hands move from F1-F6 to F7-F12 you are physically performing the mode switch between zero-focalization world-building and scene architecture. The keyboard enforces the mental separation that the data model represents. The unified OwnerControl as a reusable component with a data-template- driven top bar is architecturally sound. The note tracks themselves are identical in structure regardless of whether the owner is a Character, a PlotPoint, or a Character-to-PlotPoint link. Only the header section differs. Using data templates for the header while keeping the track rendering uniform means you write the track UI once and it works everywhere. The three-section layout within each track — Confirmed, Unset, Flagged — directly implements the stability axis as a visual hierarchy. Confirmed notes are prominent, Unset notes are present but visually receded, Flagged notes have their flag reason visible. A single glance at a track tells you its completion state without reading any content. The decision to make the link's OwnerControl available from both the subject window and the plot point window is correct for your workflow. Information genuinely flows in both directions: you start from the subject's standing truth and push design intent downward into the link tracks, and you start from a scene and populate the link tracks with what you discover during scene design. Both entry points are legitimate and neither should be privileged. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Pitfall: Three Simultaneous OwnerControls Is Cognitive Overload The three-panel layout with the left OwnerControl (subject), middle link list, and right OwnerControl (selected link) will work well in most cases but has a specific failure mode: when you open it from the plot point's perspective rather than the subject's perspective, the left panel becomes the plot point's OwnerControl and the right panel becomes the link's OwnerControl simultaneously. You now have twelve visible note tracks across two panels before you have looked at a single note. The center panel with link cards is sandwiched between two walls of content. This is manageable but requires discipline. The mitigation is making the left OwnerControl collapsible with a persistent collapse state — when you are in scene design mode, you collapse the left panel and work with the center and right panels. When you are doing arc review from the subject window, you keep the left panel open and collapse it less frequently. The collapse state persists per window instance so the UI remembers which mode you were in. A related pitfall: the three-section layout within each track (Confirmed, Unset, Flagged) multiplied across six tracks in one OwnerControl produces eighteen visual sections simultaneously. At the typical 1080p or 1440p monitor resolution, most of these sections will be empty most of the time, but the empty sections still consume vertical space. The mitigation is collapsing empty sections automatically — a track with no Flagged notes does not show the Flagged section header. A track with no Unset notes shows only the Confirmed section. Empty sections are shown only when the track is the active target for a note being classified, so you can see where a note would land. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Top Bar Template Complexity The data-template-driven top bar is the right architecture but has a scoping problem worth anticipating. The top bar for a Character subject needs to show SubjectDefinitionId-derived type badge, name, abbreviation, color, and perhaps the completion profile summary. The top bar for a PlotPoint needs chapter, order, DraftStatus, POVCharacter, and the 5 axis enum values. The top bar for a Character-to-PlotPoint link needs CharacterRole, CharacterDevImpact, ObservedGapType, IsPrimary, and LogicalOrder. These are three genuinely different data templates with different binding sources. The OwnerControl needs to know which template to select. The cleanest mechanism is a TemplateSelector on the OwnerControl that switches based on OwnerType. This is standard WPF and works well. The risk is that as you add more Subject types — World Principle, Organization, etc. — you may want type-specific top bars for links too: a World Principle-to-PlotPoint link has different relevant properties than a Character-to-PlotPoint link. The TemplateSelector needs to dispatch on both OwnerType and SubjectDefinitionId, not just OwnerType. The forward-compatible design is a TemplateSelector that first checks OwnerType for coarse routing, then checks SubjectDefinitionId for fine- grained template selection within the Subject and PlotPointSubjectLink owner types. This means the template dictionary is keyed by a composite of OwnerType and SubjectDefinitionId, with fallback to OwnerType-only when no SubjectDefinitionId-specific template exists. You only need to write specific templates for the entity types that genuinely need different top bar content. Everything else falls back to the generic template. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Link List Center Panel The center panel as a list of link cards needs one design decision made explicitly: is it ordered by LogicalOrder within the scene, or by SubjectDefinition type? Both have valid use cases. LogicalOrder reflects narrative sequence — the order in which subjects appear or are relevant in the scene. Type grouping reflects cognitive organization — all characters together, all codex entries together. The recommendation is LogicalOrder as the primary sort with a type-group toggle. Most of the time you want narrative sequence because you are designing a scene that unfolds in time. Occasionally you want type grouping to verify that all character links have their ArcStatement populated or all codex links have their UsageType assigned. A toggle at the top of the center panel switches between the two views without losing any data. Each link card needs to show enough at a glance to make the selection decision without opening the right panel: Subject name, Subject type badge, completion profile indicators (S1/S2/S3), and the single most important narrative property for scanning — CharacterRole for character links, UsageType for codex links, GoalTrajectory for thread links. These are different per link type, which means the card template is also data- template-driven by SubjectDefinitionId. The card template and the top bar template share the same dispatch logic, so writing one informs the other. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Subject Window Entry Point When the content window is opened from the subject window rather than the plot point list, the expected behavior changes subtly. From the subject window, the user is in arc review mode — they want to scan the subject's links in chronological order and open specific ones for editing. The center panel in this mode should be ordered chronologically by chapter and position, not by the scene's LogicalOrder. The left OwnerControl is the subject and stays constant as the user moves through different links. The right OwnerControl changes with each selected link, showing a different plot point's link context each time. This means the content window has two modes that share the same layout but differ in the center panel's ordering and the left panel's content. The mode is determined by the entry point: entering from a plot point gives scene design mode, entering from a subject gives arc review mode. The mode should be visually indicated — a subtle label or icon in the window title bar so you always know which mode you are in — because the function key bindings and the expected workflow are different in each. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ One Structural Improvement The current concept has the link's OwnerControl in the right panel responding to selection in the center panel. This works but creates a selection dependency — the right panel is empty until a link is selected, which means a user who opens the content window from a plot point sees two populated panels on the left and an empty right panel until they click a link card. A more active design is to auto-select the first link card when the content window opens, based on a simple heuristic: the first link with incomplete Stage 1 content. This immediately populates the right panel with the link that most needs attention and positions the cursor in the most productive starting location without requiring an explicit selection gesture. The auto-selection does not scroll the center panel aggressively — it just pre-selects the first actionable item. The user can immediately start working or can click a different link to change the selection. This heuristic — open to the first thing that needs work — is applicable throughout the application: the chapter list opens to the first chapter with incomplete scenes, the subject window opens to the first link with incomplete arc record. It converts the application from a passive document viewer into an active work management system that guides you toward the next task. The scene's subject-level design aggregates into the scene's overall specifications. The link work is the raw material, the plot point tracks are the synthesis. In the plot point window, links occupy the left panel and inherit F1-F6. The plot point's own tracks occupy the right panel and inherit F7-F12. The convention is spatial — left panel gets lower function keys, right panel gets higher — not entity-type-based. This is more intuitive than trying to remember which entity type owns which key range, because spatial position is always visible while entity type requires interpretation. The subject window keeps its current assignment because the subject is left and gets F1-F6, the link is right and gets F7-F12. Consistent with the spatial convention. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Layer-Ordered Link Sequence The proposed ordering for the plot point window's link list is: World Principles and Constructed Ideas → Characters → Story Threads → Themes This maps your materialist historicism engine onto the UI as a visual representation of the causal hierarchy. World truths constrain what is possible. Character psychology determines who acts and why within those constraints. Story threads track the goal trajectories produced by character actions within world constraints. Themes are the propositions the story argues, which emerge from the interaction of all the prior layers. This ordering works and is worth implementing for a specific reason beyond aesthetics: it matches the order in which you should design the link content. You cannot correctly design the character's ObservedGapType until you know what World Principles are operating in the scene. You cannot correctly design the thread's GoalTrajectory until you know how the characters are behaving. You cannot correctly populate EvidenceDeposited on a theme link until you know what the threads and characters are doing. The visual order in the center panel is the design dependency order. Scrolling top to bottom through the link list is walking through the design work in the correct sequence. The one refinement worth adding is a secondary sort within each layer group. Characters should be ordered by LogicalOrder within the scene — their narrative sequence within the character group. World Principle links should be ordered by UsageType, with Operational instances before Mentioned ones because operational appearances require more design work. Thread links should be ordered by IsPrimary first — the primary thread for the scene appears before supporting threads. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Complication With Two Modes The two different center panel orderings — chronological by chapter for the subject window, layer-ordered for the plot point window — are both correct for their respective cognitive tasks but they make the center panel a context-dependent component. The center panel is not one thing that displays links — it is two different views of links depending on the entry point. This is manageable if the mode is visually explicit. The window title or a persistent mode indicator should always tell you whether you are in Arc Review mode (entered from subject, links ordered chronologically) or Scene Design mode (entered from plot point, links ordered by layer). The mode label prevents the disorientation of seeing the same three-panel layout behave differently in different contexts. The implementation consequence is that the center panel's ItemsSource sort is determined at construction time from the entry point parameter, not toggled by the user during a session. You open the window in one mode or the other. You do not switch modes within an open window. If you need the other view, you open the content window from the other entry point. This keeps the mode unambiguous throughout a working session. You said: I am imagining there is only this one window for all content viewing and editing purposes. Show more May 1 Claude responded: Analysis of the Unified Content Window Analyzed hierarchical window architecture with context-sensitive panel toggling Analyzed hierarchical window architecture with context-sensitive panel toggling Analysis of the Unified Content Window The Core Insight Is Correct The single window with context-dependent panel visibility solves the mode problem cleanly. Rather than two different windows with two different layouts, you have one window whose initial state reflects the entry point. The layout is always left-entity → center-links → right- entity, but which panels are visible on open and which panel is "primary" changes based on how you arrived. Subject entry: left visible (subject), center visible (links), right hidden until link selected. Plot point entry: left hidden until link selected, center visible (links), right visible (plot point). The navigation button on each side — "go to this subject" from the plot point view, "go to this plot point" from the subject view — creates a traversal system where the window re-initializes with the new entry point's context. This is preferable to opening a second window because it keeps the application to a single focused context rather than accumulating open windows. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Navigation Button Behavior Needs Precision When you click "navigate to this plot point" from the subject view, two things need to be decided: *Does the window re-initialize or does a new window open?* Re- initializing means the subject context is lost — you cannot easily go back to where you were. Opening a new window preserves context but creates window accumulation. The better solution is a *navigation history stack* within the single window. The window maintains a back/ forward history of content states. Navigating to the plot point pushes the current subject state onto the stack and initializes the plot point view. The back button returns to the subject view with the same link pre-selected. This is the browser tab mental model applied to a desktop window — familiar, predictable, and eliminates both the lost context problem and the window accumulation problem. *Which link is pre-selected when navigating to the plot point?* The link you clicked to navigate should be pre-selected in the center panel of the plot point view. This maintains continuity — you were looking at AJ's link in the subject view, you navigate to the plot point, AJ's link card is already selected and the left panel is already open. The transition feels like zooming out from the subject-centric view to the scene-centric view of the same connection. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Collapsed Left Panel Behavior When opening from a plot point, the left panel is collapsed and appears when a link is selected. This is correct but needs one refinement: the collapse animation and the auto-select behavior interact. If you implement auto-selection of the first incomplete link (recommended earlier), the left panel will animate open immediately on window open. This is good — it eliminates the empty-right-panel-on-open problem — but it means the user sees a momentary layout shift as the left panel slides in. This is acceptable if the animation is fast (under 150ms) and if the auto-selected link is genuinely the most useful starting point. The collapsed state should persist per-session for each entry point type. If you collapse the left panel while working on a plot point, the next plot point you open should also start with it collapsed. The user is signaling a preferred working mode. A UserPreferences entry for DefaultLeftPanelState_PlotPointEntry and DefaultLeftPanelState_SubjectEntry handles this simply. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Center Panel Link Card Order In subject entry mode: chronological by chapter and position within chapter. The subject window is for arc review — you want to see the character's appearances in story order. In plot point entry mode: layer-ordered by materialist hierarchy as discussed. The plot point window is for scene design — you want to work through subjects in the order their design constrains downstream subjects. Both orderings are correct for their respective contexts. The center panel's sort is determined by entry point and does not change within a session. One addition for the subject entry center panel: a chapter group header between links from different chapters. Seeing "Chapter 4 — Magic" above the AJ link from that scene and "Chapter 7 — Generosity" above the next group makes the chronological structure scannable without reading plot point titles to orient yourself. The group header shows the chapter title and the completion summary for all links in that chapter — how many are Stage 1 complete, Stage 2 complete, Stage 3 complete. This gives you arc review at both the individual scene level (link card) and the chapter level (group header) simultaneously. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Pitfall: Center Panel Width Under Pressure With three panels in one window, the center panel is under width pressure from both sides. The left and right OwnerControls each need enough width to display note tracks readably — a track with a DisplayQuestion, three note sections, and function key labels needs at minimum 350-400px to be usable. A typical 1920px wide monitor minus two 400px OwnerControls leaves 1120px for the center panel, which is generous. On a 1280px laptop screen the same calculation leaves 480px for the center panel, which is tight for link cards with completion profile indicators and type badges. The mitigation is making all three panels resizable with a persisted split position, and making the left and right OwnerControls use a compact mode when below 380px width — hiding the section headers for empty note sections, showing only the track name and note count rather than full track content, and reserving full expansion for the active track (the one being edited). The compact mode is still functional — you can read note counts and identify which tracks need work — but takes significantly less horizontal space. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Top Bar Data Template Placement The OwnerControl's top bar — the data-template-driven section specific to Character, PlotPoint, or link type — should be the first thing visible when a panel is open and the last thing that collapses when width is reduced. It carries the entity's most critical identifying and status information: name, type, completion profile, and the primary narrative property values. When the note tracks go into compact mode due to width pressure, the top bar remains at full size. The user should always be able to see what they are looking at even when the detail content is compressed. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Floating Plot Points Plot points without a chapter assignment appear in a separate section of the main menu, as you described. These behave identically to chapter- assigned plot points in the content window — the entry point behavior is the same. The only difference is the navigation context: the back button from a floating plot point returns to the floating plot points list rather than to a chapter. This requires the navigation history stack to know the origin list, not just the origin entity, which is a small addition to the history entry structure. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Summary of What Works and What Needs Attention The single unified window with entry-point-determined initial state is the right architecture. It is coherent, navigable, and maps naturally onto both the arc review and scene design cognitive modes without requiring the user to understand the difference explicitly — the behavior follows from where they clicked. The navigation history stack is the most important addition to the design you described. Without it, the navigation buttons between subject and plot point views create a one-way trip that loses context. With it, the window becomes a traversal system through the story's connection graph, always recoverable to any prior state. The compact mode for OwnerControls under width pressure is the most important technical consideration. It determines whether the layout degrades gracefully on smaller screens or becomes unusable. Designing the compact mode before building the full mode means you know the minimum viable display requirements before committing to the layout. You said: There should not be preloading of link data. There should not be preloading of link data. Link says only appears when a link is clicked. I think there is no need for a stack or history. The link side of a view should always have a navigation button to the other side of the link. The navigation button on the non link entity is disabled. If I start on subject and click on a Link's navigation button to a plot point, the link moves to the left and the plot point appears on right. To go back, the link left button is enabled to go back to the subject. Plot point can also click on a different link and navigate to it. Now that two views can exist in one window that might remove the need for accumulation, plus better direction on what goes where instead of the arbitrary unclear dumping text fields from before Show more May 1 Claude responded: Analysis of the Link-as-Pivot Navigation Model Analyzed bidirectional navigation model with link-pivoting and click- based interaction patterns Analyzed bidirectional navigation model with link-pivoting and click- based interaction patterns Analysis of the Link-as-Pivot Navigation Model The Core Mechanic Is Elegant The link as the stable pivot point that shifts position while the flanking entity changes is a genuinely good interaction model. It solves the context problem without a history stack because the link itself is the context — it is always visible and always tells you the relationship you are looking at regardless of which side you navigated from. The state machine is simple and complete: *Subject entry state:* Subject (left) | Links (center) | Empty (right) *Link selected state:* Subject (left) | Links (center) | Link detail (right) *Navigate to plot point:* Link detail (left) | Links (center) | Plot point (right) *Navigate back via link:* Subject (left) | Links (center) | Link detail (right) The link detail sliding from right to left when you navigate to the plot point is visually meaningful — it communicates that the link is now the known context and the plot point is the new discovery. No animation is strictly required but a brief positional transition would reinforce this semantically. The disabled navigation button on the non-link entity is the correct affordance. It is always visible so the user knows the button exists, but grayed out so they understand navigation in that direction is not available from the current state. This is clearer than hiding the button entirely, which would make the UI feel inconsistent between states. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Back Navigation Is Not Symmetric There is a subtle asymmetry worth designing for explicitly. When you are in the "navigate to plot point" state — link on left, plot point on right — the back navigation via the link's button returns to the subject view. But the center panel's link list in this state is ordered by materialist layer hierarchy (plot point entry mode), not chronologically (subject entry mode). When you press back, the center panel needs to re- sort to chronological order because you are returning to subject entry mode. This re-sort should happen as part of the navigation transition, not as a separate step. The center panel's sort order is a property of which entity is in the left panel — subject on left means chronological, plot point on left means layer-ordered. The sort follows the left panel automatically. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Middle Click vs Double Click Decision Your existing muscle memory favors middle click for accumulation, which is worth preserving. The new system makes accumulation less necessary but not obsolete. There are genuine cases where you want two content windows open simultaneously — comparing two characters' link tracks side by side, or keeping a subject window open as reference while working in a plot point window. The interaction model recommendation: *Left click on a card* — navigates within the current window as described. The window content changes. This is the default and most common operation. *Middle click on a card* — opens a new content window initialized with the clicked entity. The existing window is unchanged. Windows accumulate. This preserves your existing muscle memory exactly. *Double click* — given that double click previously meant close-and- open-new, and that behavior is now handled by the single window's navigation, double click can be repurposed. The most useful repurposing is opening the entity's content window directly in the entity-centric state — double clicking a link card opens a new window with the link's subject on the left, rather than adding the link to the current window's right panel. This is the "I want to focus entirely on this subject, not within this scene's context" gesture. It is distinct from middle click, which opens the entity in whatever its natural entry state is. The three gestures then are: Gesture Effect Left click Navigate within current window Middle click Open new window, accumulate Double click Open subject-centric window for this entity ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Why Accumulation Is Less Necessary Now The two-panel layout absorbs the most common reason for accumulation in the old system: wanting to see a subject's standing truth while editing a link. In the old system this required two windows — one for the subject, one for the plot point. In the new system both are visible simultaneously in one window. The remaining cases where accumulation is genuinely useful are reference cases — keeping a World Principle subject open to verify mechanics consistency while working on a scene that uses it, or keeping Chapter 4 open as structural reference while designing Chapter 12. These are infrequent enough that middle click as an explicit gesture for them is appropriate. The default workflow should not require accumulation. The reduction in accumulation also means window management — Z-ordering, resizing, finding buried windows — becomes a rare exception rather than a daily overhead. This is a significant quality-of-life improvement that the single unified content window provides as a side effect of the better architectural decision. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Empty Right Panel on Subject Entry One remaining design question: when you first open the subject view, the right panel is empty because no link has been selected. This empty state should not be a blank gray rectangle. It should be an actionable prompt that guides the next step. The most useful content for the empty right state is the subject's completion summary — a digest view showing how many links exist, how many are at each completion stage, which chapter has the most incomplete link work, and a prompt to click a link card to begin. This converts the waiting state into a useful dashboard that motivates the first click rather than just sitting empty. When a link is selected the completion summary is replaced by the link's OwnerControl, and when the link is deselected the completion summary returns. What about a three panel layout with three owner controls, subject on left, link middle and plot point right? May 1 Claude responded: The Three-Panel Layout Is Contradictory Weighed layout contradiction against potential high-intent use cases Weighed layout contradiction against potential high-intent use cases The Three-Panel Layout Is Contradictory The contradiction you identified is real and precise. It is not a layout preference issue — it is a cognitive mode incompatibility issue. The subject's OwnerControl is a zero-focalization workspace. You are in the mode of a historian and systems engineer documenting invariant truths. The content is timeless — Applejack's psychological architecture does not change based on which scene you are looking at. The plot point's OwnerControl is a scene architecture workspace. You are in the mode of a director designing a specific reader experience in a specific moment. The content is entirely time-indexed — Stakes, ReaderState, POVBelief are meaningless outside this scene. The link's OwnerControl is the translation layer between them. Its entire purpose is to be the site where standing truth becomes scene- specific design. The Objective Record track on the link asks "what is objectively happening with this subject in this scene" — a question that requires holding the subject's standing truth in mind while making scene-specific assertions. If all three OwnerControls are simultaneously visible, the cognitive demand is to inhabit all three modes at once. This is not how design work actually proceeds. You read the subject's Track A to understand the psychology, then you move to the link to make scene-specific assertions informed by that psychology. The transition is sequential, not simultaneous. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The High-Intent Use Case That Almost Justifies It There is one genuine use case that pulls toward three simultaneous panels: *consistency verification*. When you have populated the link's Objective Record and want to confirm it is consistent with the subject's Track A and also consistent with the plot point's Stakes, seeing all three simultaneously lets you do a visual cross-check without navigating. But this is a reading task, not a writing task. You are not generating content in three cognitive modes simultaneously — you are scanning already-written content for contradictions. This is better served by a *review mode* that shows all three panels in a read-only compact display optimized for scanning, rather than a full three-panel editing layout. The review mode collapses each OwnerControl to show only Confirmed notes in a dense list — no edit controls, no empty sections, no flagged notes — so the maximum content is visible in the minimum space. The review mode is a separate interaction gesture from the editing mode. Middle click on a link card could open the three-panel review mode in a new window — showing subject, link, and plot point all at Confirmed-only density — while the main window remains in the two-panel editing mode. This gives you the consistency verification capability without contaminating the editing interface with a layout that encourages the wrong cognitive behavior during active design work. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Practical Verdict The two-panel layout with the link as pivot is correct for editing. The three-panel layout as a read-only review mode is correct for verification. They serve different tasks and should not be combined into one interface that tries to do both. The layout mistake to avoid is building the three-panel editing layout because it looks comprehensive, then discovering during use that you are constantly context-switching between incompatible cognitive modes and the richness of the layout is producing friction rather than clarity. The subject's Track A is not more useful because it is visible next to the plot point's Synopsis. It is more useful when you have read it, internalized it, and are now applying it in the link workspace with the subject panel closed. You said: The links will have a mix of note tracks for zero focalization and director modes. The links will have a mix of note tracks for zero focalization and director modes. Should their presentation adapt to context? When viewing a plot point on the right to be a director, the objective record should be read only or at least visually different to the fields that the director is supposed to fill in. When viewing a subject as primary on the left the objective record is supposed to be editable, informed by the macros architecture track primarily and also the others. maybe the director stuff should be read only or even hidden, or deemphasized May 1 Claude responded: The Principle Behind the Question Examined context-dependent UI adaptation for editable versus read-only fields Examined context-dependent UI adaptation for editable versus read-only fields The Principle Behind the Question The link's tracks serve two different work blocks and those work blocks have a natural sequence. The Objective Record and Macro Architecture tracks on the link are populated during world-building and arc design work — informed by the subject's standing truth, they document what is objectively happening with this entity in this scene. The Scene Architecture tracks (ObservedBelief, ReaderPerception, FIDAnchors) are populated during scene design work — informed by the plot point's Stakes and ReaderState, they specify how the objective reality is rendered through focalization. These are not just different cognitive modes. They are different phases of work with a dependency relationship. The Objective Record should be substantially complete before Scene Architecture work begins. The Scene Architecture tracks are meaningless without a populated Objective Record to design against. The context-adaptive presentation you are describing is the UI making this dependency visible and enforcing it through affordance rather than through rules. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Two Context Modes *Subject-primary context* (subject on left, link in center-right or right): The work being done is Layer 1-3 content flowing into Layer 4 zero- focalization assertions. The author is thinking as a historian and systems engineer. The Objective Record track is the primary writing target. Macro Architecture notes are secondary — they govern how the objective content is disclosed across the story. Presentation response: * Objective Record track — fully editable, visually prominent, appearing first * Macro Architecture track — editable, secondary prominence * Scene Architecture tracks — visible but read-only with a clear visual treatment indicating they belong to a different work phase. Not hidden, because seeing that they exist and are empty is a useful diagnostic signal — it tells you this link has pending Scene Architecture work. But grayed out or behind a visual separator labeled something like "Scene Design — complete in plot point context" * Narrative property assignments (CharacterRole, ObservedGapType) — editable for the structural ones (CharacterRole is a zero- focalization assertion), read-only for the scene architecture ones (ObservedGapType requires plot point context to populate correctly) *Plot point-primary context* (link on left, plot point on right): The work being done is zero-focalization assertions informing Scene Architecture design. The author is thinking as a director. The Scene Architecture tracks are the primary writing target. The Objective Record is reference material. Presentation response: * Objective Record track — read-only, visually receded, appearing below a separator. Content is visible because the director needs to read it as the foundation for scene architecture work. But no edit controls — if you notice an error in the Objective Record you navigate back to the subject context to fix it * Macro Architecture track — read-only, same treatment as Objective Record. Reference material for the director * Scene Architecture tracks — fully editable, visually prominent, appearing first above the separator * Narrative property assignments — editable for scene architecture ones (ObservedGapType), read-only display for structural ones already set in subject context ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Visual Language The separator between the two groups of tracks should carry the cognitive mode label explicitly. Something like: |━━━━━━━━ OBJECTIVE RECORD ━━━━━━━━ [zero-focalization tracks, read-only in plot point context] ━━━━━━━━ SCENE DESIGN ━━━━━━━━ [scene architecture tracks, read-only in subject context]| The read-only tracks should not look disabled — disabled implies broken or unavailable. They should look like reference material. A slightly different background, no edit controls visible, but text fully readable. The distinction is: you can act on this (editable) versus you are reading this to inform your actions elsewhere (reference). The CognitiveMode enum on NoteTrackDefinition already distinguishes ZeroFocalization from SceneArchitecture. This is the exact field that drives the context-adaptive presentation. The OwnerControl receives a ContextMode parameter — SubjectPrimary or PlotPointPrimary — and uses it combined with each track's CognitiveMode to determine editability and visual treatment. csharp |public enum LinkContextMode { SubjectPrimary, // Editing objective record, scene design is reference PlotPointPrimary // Editing scene design, objective record is reference } // In NoteTrackViewModel, given the context mode: public bool IsEditable => ContextMode == LinkContextMode.SubjectPrimary ? Definition.CognitiveMode == CognitiveMode.ZeroFocalization || Definition.CognitiveMode == CognitiveMode.Metatextual : Definition.CognitiveMode == CognitiveMode.SceneArchitecture || Definition.CognitiveMode == CognitiveMode.LinguisticExecution;| ------------------------------------------------------------------------ Why Not Hide the Read-Only Tracks Hiding the scene architecture tracks in subject context removes the diagnostic signal that Scene Architecture work is pending. If the tracks are hidden, a fully populated Objective Record looks complete when it is actually only half-done. The empty Scene Architecture tracks — visible but grayed out — communicate "this work exists and has not been done" without demanding that it be done right now. They are a reminder in the periphery that becomes the focus when you switch to plot point context. The same argument applies in reverse — hiding the Objective Record in plot point context removes your ability to read the foundation while doing scene architecture work. A director who cannot see the zero- focalization truth they are translating into focalized experience is working blind. The reference material must be present even when it is not the editing target. ------------------------------------------------------------------------ The Metatextual Track Special Case The Metatextual CognitiveMode — the Development Notes and Project Management track — is editable in both contexts. It belongs to neither the historian nor the director. It belongs to the author-as-project- manager, who may want to add an obligation or a cross-reference note regardless of which context they are currently working in. The Metatextual tracks should be visually separated from both groups — perhaps below both — and always editable, always the same visual treatment regardless of context mode.
